# Milestone task tracker

Last updated: 2026-09-16T17:00:00+10:00

This tracker follows the roadmap. It records implementation work, discovered defects, owners, exact acceptance scope, and the evidence needed to close each gate.

Status values are deliberately narrow:

- verified local: the stated scope passed at its recorded revision or baseline; this does not
  imply hosted CI, production readiness, or a broader milestone gate.
- implemented awaiting verification: source changes exist and a focused
  result or component path exists, but the required rerun or wider scope is
  still open.
- in progress: active implementation or an active integration run remains.
- planned: not started.
- blocked: cannot close until the named prerequisite or external dependency
  changes.

The milestone summary is a gate view, not a count of checked boxes. A
milestone remains open until every required task has evidence at its declared
scope.

M1 and M2 summary statuses preserve the historical local baselines stated in
their evidence documents and are intentionally narrower than the current
rerun rows. M1-01..03 and M2-01..04 describe those dated baseline results;
M1-04, M1-05, M2-05 and M2-06 track current or broader work. A later code,
fixture, authorization, or harness change does not erase a historical result,
but it does keep the affected current row open until its exact command and
scope are rerun.

## Completion timing

`Completed at` is the time the row's full stated acceptance scope was verified,
not the time coding finished or an estimate of future completion. Record new
completions as ISO 8601 timestamps with seconds and an explicit offset, using
Australia/Brisbane (`+10:00`), in the same update as status and evidence. Open
rows use `—`. Milestone timestamps require the full milestone gate to pass.

Historical dates come from [m1-harness.md](m1-harness.md),
[m2-verification.md](m2-verification.md), and
[m7-verification.md](m7-verification.md). `time not recorded` means only the
date is known; it must not be treated as midnight or included in hourly rates.
Use `unknown (historical)` if no completion date can be established. The
baseline below was observed when timing was added; it does not date those
historical completions or assert that current reruns passed.

Append each new task, first completion, reopening, and re-verification to
[Completion history](#completion-history), with its stable ID and evidence.
On reopening, preserve the previous completion in history and clear the row's
`Completed at`; on re-verification, record a new timestamp. Keep history
append-only, recording corrections as new events. Count first completions
separately from re-verifications to measure tasks completed per hour/day;
reopenings reduce net completion and new tasks increase scope. These timestamps
measure completion throughput, not hands-on effort or test execution duration.
Apply the same rules to the [edge-case matrix](m7-edge-cases.md#completion-history).

## M7 — relay cluster, Redis membership, owner routing, and integration

The component rows below are intentionally separate from the integration rows.
The 98-row edge-case gate remains tracked in
[m7-edge-cases.md](m7-edge-cases.md); that matrix is linked evidence coverage,
not a completed task.

Each M7 row links its **Edge-case tests** to the
[task-specific test scope](m7-task-tests.md). Test every referenced case against
its complete matrix requirements; linked component evidence or a shared passing
fixture cannot automatically close the broader case. The index preserves
partial contributions and identifies the additional integration evidence needed.

| M7-C06 | [x] Verify cluster startup/readiness wiring, signed checkpoint refresh, configured Redis TLS, dynamic pins, and separate liveness/readiness. | verified local | w-c06 and w-spki workers, root validation | Configured dependency restoration passes in 32.97 s, integrated as 94695bb (the worker commit it was first measured on was never merged, so its hash is deliberately not cited): the pre-fault device session is closed by the relay's typed `AUTHORITY_UNAVAILABLE` maintenance close (exact `owner_check_failed`/`session_closed` diagnostics for that session, epoch and device; the client reports the exact `control read`/`control socket closed` transport close only in that stage), admission returns the `CLUSTER_UNREADY` boundary, same-PID readiness recovers, the stale owner clears only by lease expiry, and a fresh fenced session with owner epoch 2 serves the authenticated echo over configured `rediss://`. Root cause was the catalog's one-shot multiplexed connections: a severed Redis connection was permanent until process restart. `RedisCatalog` now wraps each physical connection in an `AuthorityLane` that fails the discovering command closed without replay, reconnects only for a later command after repeating the PING/INFO identity check, refuses a changed `run_id` with a typed conflict, and shares a loss generation so sibling lanes probe before their next caller command; fake-authority unit tests and the Redis-backed `redis_lane_reconnect` severing-proxy test cover it. Sibling gates `m7_deployment_runtime_faults` 2/2 (11.22 s) and `m7_deployment_redis_stages` 1/1 (16.90 s) pass; all 31 explicit Redis cases pass in the worker tree. Dynamic configured peer-certificate replacement is now proven without restart, integrated as 78d46c2 and passing three consecutive times in about 30 seconds: two configured relay processes with separate TLS-forwarded Redis and a live signing checkpoint authority, where the subject relay is never restarted while the signed record for its peer walks the retired key, then both keys during overlap, then the replacement. The retired certificate is accepted before the record changes and during the overlap and rejected afterwards; the replacement is rejected before its record exists, accepted during the overlap and afterwards; an unapproved certificate is rejected throughout; readiness stays ready across 27 overlap samples with none unready; a stale record publish is refused; and after the pin is retired the subject becomes unready, releases the owner, closes the device session and returns the typed `503 CLUSTER_UNREADY`/`not_dispatched` on that route. An impostor QUIC server presenting the retired certificate at the peer endpoint after the overlap receives one connection from the subject and is closed without a stream. Under an unsigned record the subject goes unready and rejects every certificate, and after a fresh signed record it recovers and accepts only the replacement. A deterministic membership walk test also proves a rollback to the overlap record cannot re-approve the retired key. Two documented boundaries: the typed public outcome across a retired route is the readiness outcome rather than a peer-trust outcome because readiness withdraws the route before peer resolution, and a restarted relay needs every node's catalog record above its persisted fence by design. Corrected 2026-09-12 after an independent assessment: this row's dynamic-pin evidence came from `m7_deployment_spki_replacement`, which is `#[ignore]`d and was never registered in `scripts/m7-harness-verify.sh`, so it has not run since 78d46c2 despite four later commits touching `peer_runtime.rs`/`tunnel-transport/src/peer.rs`; it is now registered as a gate and its first current-source result is pending. The test's own header also over-claimed a post-replacement public canary, which phase 5 explicitly does not exercise; the header now states the real scope. Still open for this row: a device session and consumer request actually served by the replaced process (the readiness-convergence limitation, not M7-C55, which is verified); and process-level Redis restart or a changed run identifier is unit-tested only. **Edge-case tests:** [FP-10, EC-010, EC-011, EC-013, EC-015, EC-018, EC-019, EC-048, EC-051, EC-055, OG-07](m7-task-tests.md#m7-c06). **Verified 2026-09-15 on both remaining clauses, each against a real process rather than a fake.** The replacement clause is no longer a readiness-convergence limitation: after the relay process is replaced, a fresh device session attaches to the listener that process owns and a public consumer request is served end to end and compared byte-exact against a canary. That the replacement served it is checkable rather than asserted, because the owner claim carries the same node and deployment incarnation with a different boot identifier, a fresh session and a strictly higher epoch, and the device generation is unchanged across the canary. Four consecutive runs pass, with four red controls covering a substituted owner, a mutated canary, an inverted survival flag and a perturbed generation. The Redis clause is a true process restart rather than a run-identifier substitute: a dedicated pinned loopback container is restarted while one catalog stays connected, so that catalog meets a genuinely new run identifier on a live socket. Every command after the restart is refused with the typed run-identifier conflict, never a value and never another shape, and a catalog connected after the restart reads the same seeded data back, which is what makes the refusal identity-specific rather than a dead connection. Registered as a gate and passing in the full suite. **Two latent fixture defects were fixed on the way**, both of which had been making the phase weaker than its own comments claimed: the first relay's signed record was signed once at fixture build and aged past its bound mid-run while the comment already claimed both nodes were re-issued, and the attach and canary were sampled once rather than converged inside the existing bounded deadline. **Product observation recorded, not changed:** a catalog failure inside consumer authentication maps to `401 UNAUTHORIZED` with `not_dispatched` instead of the `AUTHORIZATION_UNAVAILABLE` 503 boundary defined a few lines above it, so an availability failure reaches a consumer as an authorization rejection and is indistinguishable at the HTTP boundary from a real credential rejection. The gate bounds and counts it rather than tolerating it silently, and all four final runs observed none. Also observed and backed out rather than worked around: republishing a node's own signed record while the canary window is open destabilises that relay's readiness for over twenty seconds. | 2026-09-15T17:40:00+10:00 |

Load-bearing was proven twice over, at runtime by each mutant leaving exactly one unsatisfied condition and physically by deleting each of the ten guards in turn, which failed the suite every time. On the M2 side the continuous-traffic validator was already pure and already named its failures but had nothing proving any of its eleven conditions could fail, which is coverage-shaped rather than coverage; it now has a complete fixture, one isolating case per condition, a separate case for the rotation requirement of zero, and eleven of eleven guards confirmed load-bearing by neutralising each in turn. A pure validator would require either retaining response bodies in the evidence, which violates the payload-free diagnostics rule this repository enforces, or reducing each comparison to a boolean at capture time, which moves the real check into untested capture code and leaves the validator re-checking a boolean. Its conditions already have helper-level coverage at the level they actually live: the named, status and echo assertions are pure and have accept and reject cases including body and status mismatches, with a case asserting no body bytes reach the diagnostic. **Recorded, not fixed:** the cluster evidence populates relay_count, tenant_count and device_count and validates none of them. | M7-C22 | [x] Eliminate ACK feedback and preserve bounded carrier control delivery during saturation, rotation and terminal cleanup. | verified local | w-late and w-fence workers, root validation | Late DATA/FIN passes as integrated at b3b0c21; the superseded pre-integration citation is dropped. Root cause: after the connector's late FIN the owner replies with its own FIN, the device ACKs it in the same round trip, and `flush_owner_stream_forgets` queues STREAM_FORGET and removes the stream inside that actor transaction, so no polling observer can see the terminal stream; the existing first-transition terminal latch may predate the connector FIN and cannot serve as that frame's receipt. The relay now publishes a separate bounded `StreamTerminalReceiptEvent` ring (32 entries, deduplicated on the complete owner/carrier/stream/request identity) when an authenticated connector FIN/RESET is accepted, before the terminal reply and the FORGET flush, recording the exact carrier, final receive/delivery cursors and terminal sequence, the reverse-direction emitted/acked cursors, retained replay bytes and queue bytes; it is payload-free, survives reclamation and never keeps a stream alive. The fixture accepts the live exact terminal stream or that exact receipt, and stream absence without a matching receipt remains a hard failure. Gate evidence: `terminal_evidence_source=stream_terminal_receipt terminal_stream_latched=true owner_recv_after=2 owner_delivered_after=2 owner_receive_terminal_sequence=2 consumer_terminal=close cleanup_joined=true`. Scope decision recorded after investigation: the typed owner-send rejection is deliberately **not** part of this gate's evidence, because it is unreachable in this scenario rather than merely slow. The harness already runs a 10-second QUIC idle timeout (`production_cluster.rs` builds the peer limits with `with_timeouts(10s, 5s)`; `ClusterConfig::peer_idle_timeout_seconds` is membership-only and reaches the transport only in the production binary), and the owner's small forwarded response buffers into the live stream's send window and returns success, after which STREAM_FORGET reclaims the stream within milliseconds and the handler exits through registration closure. Two thousand six hundred fifty-seven owner snapshots across 66 seconds recorded no owner-send or owner-receive diagnostic. Forcing one would require deliberately holding the late response past connection death, which risks tearing the stream down before the late frame and so destroying the receipt evidence itself. The bounded receipt gives positive owner-side proof and the interrupted consumer proves non-delivery. Configured message-queue saturation is no longer open: `verify-m7-queue-saturation` exists and passes 54 checks on the current source. Corrected 2026-09-12 after an independent assessment, the remaining clause for this row is narrower than the old text implied: the saturation gate proves free byte headroom reserved for control, cancellation, revocation and rotation, but no revocation close and no late, duplicate or reordered DATA/credit frame is actually delivered on the saturated carrier, so bounded control delivery under saturation is proven by reservation rather than by delivery. Corrected 2026-09-15: the clause is closed. A real `AUTHORIZATION_INVALIDATED` revocation close is delivered on the bounded control channel while the data channel holds 52 of its 128 slots, and it is acted on rather than merely enqueued: the owner latches the stream terminal with the typed `GRANT_UNAVAILABLE` authorization failure code and dispatches nothing (`revocation_dispatch_delta=0`). Reordered, duplicate and post-FIN DATA frames and connector acknowledgement frames are all actually delivered on that same saturated carrier. `verify-m7-saturated-peer-frames` (`crates/tunnel-test-harness/src/peer_frames/saturated.rs`) closes the conjunction on one genuinely saturated non-owner-ingress route. It builds on the EC-044 fixture, which terminates the connector side itself, because the queue-saturation gate's carrier is a real CLI's mTLS socket behind a TCP proxy: forged, reordered or post-FIN frames cannot be placed on a TLS session the fixture does not terminate, and a device carrier has no peer GOAWAY seam. The device data carrier runs on its own peer client whose QUIC receive windows are deliberately small (192 KiB connection, 128 KiB stream - an environment bound, the analogue of the queue-saturation gate's 1 KiB proxy receive buffer), so when the fixture stops reading, the owner's physical writer blocks and its bounded outbound data channel fills; control and every consumer stream run on a second, ordinarily windowed connection. The unchanged queue-saturation workload is used: 20,000-byte records, one in-flight record per stream, the full 64-stream per-device cap proven by a refusal at cap plus one, and a required physical residency floor of `max_queue_messages / 4`. Evidence from four consecutive runs: `streams_admitted=64 stream_cap_refused_one_more=true data_queue_depth_high_water=52 physically_resident_frames=53 resident_floor=32 min_resident_during_injection=46..52 reserved_free_data_slots_at_peak=76 queue_bytes_high_water=2244475 data_bytes_high_water=2244475 control_bytes_available_at_data_peak=1949829 carrier_drain_records_consumed=14..22/48 saturation_window_ms=655..782/20000 revocation_close_delivered=true revocation_close_reason=grant-unavailable revocation_failure_code=GRANT_UNAVAILABLE revocation_stream_terminal=true revocation_dispatch_delta=0 data_queue_depth_at_revocation=52 control_enqueues_during_saturation=1 control_queue_refusals_during_saturation=0 reorder_recv_after_gap=0 reorder_delivered_after_gap=0 reorder_delivered_after_fill=2 reorder_order_restored=true duplicate_delivered_before=1 duplicate_delivered_after=1 duplicate_adapter_records=1 no_double_delivery=true late_fin_cursor=2 late_stream_terminals=1 late_adapter_bytes_after_fin=0 late_session_reason=INVALID_SEQUENCE terminal_identity_immutable=true terminal_identity_samples=8 sibling_survived_terminals=true goaway_sent_by_owner=true goaway_refused_new_peer_stream=true goaway_refusal_class=goaway`. Six live red controls fail without their assertion: delivering the reordered pair in order (`reorder_recv_after_gap,reorder_delivered_after_gap`), withholding the post-FIN frame (the session never closes), leaving the grant live (no revocation close arrives), never requesting the planned drain (the owner never reports a GOAWAY), dropping the workload to four fill streams (the residency floor is not reached), and replacing the duplicate with a genuinely new sequence (the session fences `INVALID_SEQUENCE` as unsolicited). A 53-case and an 18-case C17 validator mutation test cover every flag, count and bound. Two structural facts are recorded rather than worked around. `handle_peer_device_data` services one forwarded carrier from a single `tokio::select!` loop, so while its physical write is parked on QUIC flow control it is not reading: a fully blackholed forwarded carrier cannot deliver an inbound frame at all. The gate therefore consumes a bounded, counted drain allowance (at most 48 already-queued frames) only when an injected frame has not yet been accounted, and asserts the *minimum* residency observed at every injection step stayed above the same floor, so saturation is measured continuously rather than once. And `invalidate_stream_challenge` arms a five-second fail-closed terminal-FIN window that clears only when the stream is physically removed, so the fixture answers the revocation close the way a connector does, with its own `FIN` and an acknowledgement of the owner's terminal. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-028, EC-029, EC-030, EC-032, EC-036, EC-037, EC-038, EC-039, EC-044, EC-047, EC-057, EC-058, EC-060, IN-02, IN-06](m7-task-tests.md#m7-c22). **Product behaviour recorded 2026-09-15, reviewed by the repository owner and deliberately left unchanged for M7.** Revoking a consumer grant on a live stream arms a five-second fail-closed terminal-FIN window that clears only on physical stream removal, and the forget proof requires an acknowledged terminal in both directions. If the connector does not answer that revocation close promptly, the relay fails the **entire device session** closed with `TERMINAL_FIN_TIMEOUT`, not just the revoked stream, so a slow or busy connector loses every stream on that device. The saturated-frames gate is the evidence of what actually happens: its fixture has to answer the revocation the way a connector does, with its own FIN and an acknowledgement of the owner's terminal, or the session fails closed. Whether that blast radius should be narrowed to the revoked stream is a change to fail-closed semantics and is post-M7 design work, not a silent fix during an evidence task. | 2026-09-15T11:21:41+10:00 |

| M7-C31 | [x] Bound listener connection permits with an HTTP header-read/idle deadline so idle TLS connections cannot exhaust accepts. | verified local | w-c31 worker, root validation | Integrated as aa70ffb. The listener installs a Tokio timer on both the HTTP/1 and HTTP/2 builders (without which hyper silently discards its own header-read deadline) and adds its own pre-request deadline covering version sniffing, the HTTP/2 preamble and the first request head, because `hyper_util`'s protocol sniffer waits for the 24-byte HTTP/2 preface with no deadline and HTTP/2 has no header-read timeout. The pre-request deadline is disarmed permanently by the first request dispatched to the router, observed through a thin service wrapper at the exact phase boundary, so established device WebSocket upgrades, in-flight consumer requests and streaming response bodies are untouched. The three bounds are a validated `ListenerTimeouts` value with documented defaults of 10 s handshake, 15 s pre-request and 10 s HTTP/1 header read, a 100 ms to 300 s range per field and the cross-field rule that the header-read bound does not exceed the pre-request bound; an invalid value returns a typed error and releases the listener. The permit count stays at 64, so a silent connection holds a permit for at most 25 s. Red-then-green real-socket regressions: filling all 64 permits with handshaken-but-silent TLS connections, asserting a further connection is refused, then that every silent socket reaches EOF inside the bound, all permits can be retaken and a further connection is served; plus an idle keep-alive closure case. The guard test proving an established request survives well past the bound passed before and after. Six configuration unit tests cover defaults, both inclusive boundaries per field, zero values and the cross-field rule. Transport 38 tests and relay 262 tests pass, and the M1 acceptance passes with five clients. Remaining exposure, recorded as a narrower follow-up: an HTTP/2 connection that dispatches one request and then idles still holds a permit, since hyper has no HTTP/2 idle deadline and adding PING keep-alive would change wire behaviour; and `ListenerTimeouts` is not yet surfaced in the relay TOML. **Edge-case tests:** [FP-10, EC-028, EC-032](m7-edge-cases.md#failure-policy-summary-rows). | 2026-09-11T20:02:30+10:00 |
| M7-C32 | [x] Keep per-session maintenance authority reads off the single serialized catalog connection. | verified local | w-c32 worker, root validation | Integrated as c9f3880. `AuthorityLane` no longer holds its mutex across the caller's command: the lock is held only for the bounded probe and reconnect that verifies the physical connection, then the caller gets a clone of the multiplexed connection so commands pipeline on one socket and the two-second deadline bounds Redis rather than the queue. A new connection generation guards loss release, so concurrent observers release a lane exactly once and a late observer cannot release a connection a sibling already re-established; the same-primary, no-replay and typed run-identifier conflict contract is unchanged. Two further real defects were found and fixed in passing: redis-rs applies its own per-command response timeout defaulting to 500 ms, below the documented bound, so stalls were reported at 500 ms until the connection was configured with the documented operation timeout; and the maintenance diagnostic's category check used a source-chain downcast that never matched because redis-rs wraps the io error, so every stall was mislabelled as transport loss instead of a timeout, which is exactly the distinction M7-C14 claims. Two dedicated maintenance lanes (seven total) now serve device resolution and owner renewal round-robin, and the tick starts at most 64 authority operations, renewals by oldest lease first and then identity re-checks rotated by cursor, so every session is visited within a bounded number of ticks and renewals stay ahead of a 30-second lease above the default device cap; lease safety is unchanged, so an unconfirmed renewal still fences. Red-then-green: twenty queued callers with 150 ms replies previously failed seven times after the two-second deadline; a new Redis-backed maintenance-queue regression with 100 ms replies previously failed 35 of 40 resolutions and 31 of 40 renewals; the category test previously returned transport loss. Catalog 48, relay 250 and startup 6 tests pass with four explicit Redis suites, and the Redis partition and production gates pass. Recorded residual risk: the 64 bound and lane count are proven by unit and catalog-level regressions rather than a relay gate at thousands of sessions, the bound is a compile-time constant, the regression models added reply latency rather than a saturated server, and the startup fixture's eight-connection cap is now tight against seven lanes. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-011, EC-013, IN-05](m7-edge-cases.md#numbered-edge-case-inventory). | 2026-09-11T21:39:10+10:00 |
| M7-C33 | [x] Scope consumer admission permits per tenant/owner and fail a mismatched device challenge promptly. | verified local | w-c33 worker, root validation | Integrated as ce8e97a. Consumer admission now reserves a per-owner permit scoped to the canonical `(tenant_id, device_id)` owner scope underneath the existing relay-global bound: that is exactly the owner an admitted operation targets and the scope already used by owner resolution, the durable catalog keys and the owner token, its cardinality is bounded by `max_devices`, and it prevents both a device starving its tenant's other devices and a tenant multiplying its allowance by minting principals. The new `max_pending_operations_per_owner` is validated 1..=64 with a default of 48, clamped to the global bound, so at least a quarter of relay ingress can never be taken by one tenant and device while one owner retains headroom for its 64-stream allowance spread across ingress relays. Refusals reuse the existing `ADMISSION_LIMIT`/`not_dispatched` outcome with its bounded retry hint through one shared helper, and the permit is an RAII guard released exactly once on drop, covering abandoned requests, refused peer admission, cancelled upgrades and normal close; scope entries exist only while a permit is held, so the registry stays bounded. Separately `begin_device_challenge` no longer ignores a mismatched challenge answer: it records the rejecting record and invalidates the pending operation or stream through the existing paths, so the stream fails promptly with the existing closed-vocabulary code instead of waiting for its full deadline. Red-then-green: with the per-owner reservation removed, tenant B received 429 instead of 101 while tenant A held its allowance; with the actor fix reverted, both mismatch regressions failed on the typed code and promptness across all three mismatch kinds. Relay 242 plus 31 tests pass, and `verify-m7-admission`, `verify-m7-concurrent-load` (128 attempts, 64 accepted, 63 typed over-cap refusals, zero unknown outcomes) and `verify-m7-public-abandoned-upgrade` pass. Recorded residual exposure: two tenants can still collectively reach the process bound, the device and service listing routes take only the global permit for one bounded read, and the bound is per relay process so cluster-wide fairness would need shared accounting. **Edge-case tests:** [FP-09, EC-028, EC-030](m7-edge-cases.md#failure-policy-summary-rows). | 2026-09-11T20:22:40+10:00 |
| M7-C34 | [x] Upgrade jsonwebtoken to 10.3.0 and re-verify strict consumer JWT validation. | verified local | w-jwt worker, root validation | Integrated as f87034f. jsonwebtoken 9.3.1 → 10.3.0 with the `rust_crypto` backend in relay, catalog and harness (10.x ships no crypto backend by default); Cargo.lock moves only jsonwebtoken plus its 20 new RustCrypto transitive crates. Configuration-time key bounds replace ring's implicit verify-time checks: RSA modulus 2048..=4096 bits with odd exponent ≥ 3, Ed25519 keys exactly 32 raw bytes, key family bound to the approved algorithm; JWKS import approves only RS256 RSA keys of verifiable size. | M7-C48 | [x] Fail closed on an over-limit length prefix and complete maximum-size bodies on the remote consumer ingress. | verified local | w-prefix worker, root validation | Integrated as 9ec2452. Root cause: the forwarding ingress only checked each public frame against the body bound and never parsed the length prefix, so a frame carrying a prefix one byte above the limit was forwarded to the owner, where the owner-side parse collapsed over-limit and not-yet-complete into one wait branch; the public stream stayed open, the owner counted a body read and the stream died only when the peer idle timeout cancelled it. A new shared consumer-framing module now holds the single bounded decision for all three paths, rejecting an over-limit prefix as soon as four bytes are complete, before any allocation or peer forwarding, and the forwarding cursor holds at most three bytes of an incomplete prefix so a split prefix is decided rather than forwarded blind. The second symptom was not a framing defect: maximum-size bodies on the remote route are byte-exact and complete, and what failed was a stream left idle across the fixture's ten-second peer idle bound, now pinned as explicit evidence. Red-then-green: the new gate failed with the prefix not closed, one owner read, two reads for the split case and a lost sibling; it now passes with the over-limit and split cases closed immediately with zero owner reads and zero dispatches, maximum and repeated maximum bodies exact, zero-body exact, truncated held open, coalesced exact, an over-budget coalesced frame closed with zero reads, the sibling refreshed and surviving, and joined cleanup. Fourteen boundary cases are asserted identical for the local and remote paths, and the gate has a 36-case mutation test. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-031, EC-034, EC-049, EC-053, EC-054](m7-edge-cases.md#numbered-edge-case-inventory). | 2026-09-12T00:05:10+10:00 |

The reservation is carved out of the existing session budget rather than adding a second budget, so the total bound stays one number: four reserved control slots at the 32 KiB control-message cap reserve 131,072 bytes, data reservations (pending bodies, retained replay chunks, encoded data frames and reassembled response payloads) are refused above 4,063,232, and control reservations are refused only at the full 4 MiB. Four slots cover a rotation's back-to-back recovery pair plus a cancellation and a revocation close, and the configured 256 KiB minimum queue budget is exactly twice the reservation. Data refusals keep their existing shape. Red-then-green: with the reservation forced to zero, a rotation recovery begin, a recovery closed, a cancellation and a revocation close were all refused at the saturated byte budget while 128 control slots were still free, proving slots were never the constraint; with the reservation in place all four enqueue and data is refused at the boundary. The saturation gate's validator now derives residency from the data byte limit, requires the data high-water never to touch the reserve, and requires the control bytes available at the data peak to be at least the reservation, with eight new mutation cases in the gate table and three in the command table. Gate evidence: `control_reserved_bytes=131072 data_bytes_limit=4063232 data_bytes_high_water=2304044 control_bytes_available_at_data_peak=1890260 control_refusals=0 control_enqueues_during_blackhole=12 streams_admitted=64 rotations_after_drain=3`. Recorded residual risk: the data high-water latches the total charge at a data admission, so it understates available control bytes conservatively rather than being an exact data-only peak; the live run exercises availability but not data refusal exactly at the boundary, which the deterministic test covers; and the four-slot reservation is a judgement call with a single knob. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-028, EC-030, EC-039](m7-edge-cases.md#numbered-edge-case-inventory). | 2026-09-12T00:22:40+10:00 |

| M7-C54 | [x] Prove convergence from the bounded membership refresh when the invalidation hint is dropped (EC-019, EC-048, EC-056). | verified local | w-c54 worker, root validation | Scoped by the key and recovery group and deliberately left to avoid colliding with the peer-pin replacement work: there is no Redis Pub/Sub in this codebase, so membership distribution is poll-only with a bounded refresh and the "missed hint" is the process-local invalidation callback. Install a callback that drops the rotated identity, require convergence from the bounded refresh alone, keep an established stream alive across the overlap, and reject the withdrawn key afterwards with a typed outcome and no dispatch. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-019, EC-048, EC-056](m7-edge-cases.md#numbered-edge-case-inventory). Design review 2026-09-12: the injection point already exists (`MembershipRuntime::set_invalidation_callback`), so a harness-only filtering callback can drop the hint for the rotated identity. Product risk to check before building: pin publication happens only inside that callback at both install sites, so if the hint is dropped nothing republishes pins until the next dispatch; if the reconcile pass does not itself withdraw the key, this row needs a product change (republish at the end of each reconcile) rather than a gate. The gate must also assert against the membership refresh bound, not the reconcile tick. **Correction 2026-09-14, with file:line evidence:** the design review's product risk was wrong and no product change is needed. It conflated two surfaces. Admission does not read the SPKI pin set at all: `admit_peer` binds against `state.verifier`, and `reconcile_once_inner` swaps that verifier in under the state lock, so a reconcile pass by itself refuses a withdrawn key. Reconcile also calls `revalidate_active`, which re-binds every live admission against the new verifier and computes the invalidation itself; the callback is only the notification of that, and at both install sites its body does nothing but republish pins. The serving binary republishes pins on its own timer independently of the callback, and the harness leaves pin publication callback-exclusive deliberately so the existing key-revocation fixture can hold a manual withdrawal until it restores it, which the proposed change would have broken. So this row is a gate: a harness-only filtering callback drops the hint for one identity, suppressing only the eager pin republication, and leaves the reconciled verifier to refuse. Scope set after that answer: prove both that a stream on the rotated identity survives while both keys are valid and is torn down only once the old key leaves the verifier, and that a stream on an unrelated identity is untouched throughout, with the refusal measured on a fresh admission and asserted against the 20-second refresh bound while the observed convergence near the 1-second reconcile tick is recorded separately. Built and integrated as 7f5be40: `verify-m7-membership-hint-drop`, taking the suite to 74 gates, harness-only with no product edits. Two implementation findings mattered more than the gate itself. The hint drop had to be window-wide rather than filtered to the rotated identity, because pin publication at both install sites is all-or-nothing over the whole verified snapshot, so a hint delivered for any other identity would republish the rotated identity's pins and silently defeat the drop; the evidence records both that the target's hints were dropped and that no publication at all ran in the window, and the validator requires that count to be zero. And the typed refusal is `PEER_UNTRUSTED`/`not_dispatched`, not the `CLUSTER_UNREADY` trust-expiry produces, because the ingress relay's own membership is untouched by this rotation so it answers about the peer and names the withdrawn key; the gate requires exactly that and explicitly rejects the two weaker variants, since a refusal blaming the ingress or a transient reachability fault would not prove the rotation caused it. The overlap arm needed no new barrier: both keys are visible in each relay's verifier at the overlap version, so the established stream completes an exchange with the overlap confirmed open and fails closed only once the old key leaves, exercising `revalidate_active` re-binding the pooled admission rather than a pin-watcher close. Evidence: 2 of 2 on the integrated branch plus 3 in the worker tree, `withdrawn_outcome=PeerUntrusted`, `publications_in_window=0`, `overlap_stream_survived=true`, `overlap_stream_interrupted=true`, `sibling_stream_survived=true`, `dispatch_unchanged=true`, convergence observed near 0.9 s and asserted against the 20-second refresh bound with the reconcile tick recorded separately and never asserted on. Recorded caveat: `fanout_peak_open` is 0 because both connector profiles pin directly to their owner's device listener, deliberately, so that ceiling is validated with no pressure against it here. | 2026-09-14T11:47:00+10:00 |
| M7-C55 | [x] Make peer readiness converge when a relay boots while its peer is flapping. | verified local | w-readiness worker, root validation | Confirmed a real product defect and fixed, integrated as e458657. Probe admission depended on the receiving relay's own readiness in two places: the accept path gated on the membership runtime's readiness, and the peer refresh loop replaced the required route set with an empty set whenever membership readiness dropped, erasing the approved pin set. A relay that was reachable but momentarily unready therefore refused the probe and the handler dropped the stream with no response headers, which is exactly the `H3_FRAME_UNEXPECTED` rejection reported as "stream finished without receiving response headers". Route readiness was tracking the peer's readiness rather than its reachability, so a relay beside a starting, degraded or flapping peer could not converge. The worker also corrected the original description: the coupling is one-directional rather than a circular deadlock, which is why it presented as non-convergence across a flap rather than a permanent hang. Reachability is now measured independently of the responder's readiness, and withdrawal drops reachability and capacity evidence and fences the revision so readiness and admission still fail closed at once, while keeping the verified route and pin set; that set is dropped only when no approved key material could be published. Every fail-closed property is preserved: mutual TLS against approved pins, the relay role, a node identifier and key matching a currently verified route, no route set meaning every probe is refused, and one bounded payload-free stream with no owner, ticket or dispatch state. Red-then-green: three regressions failed before the fix, covering convergence while cluster readiness is withdrawn, convergence across a flap, and withdrawal dropping stale probe evidence; two further tests prove an unapproved peer is still refused while withdrawn and that a probe is refused with no known route set. Relay 303 tests pass, the peer readiness and capacity gates pass, and the configured certificate-replacement test passes. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-011, FP-10, OG-08](m7-edge-cases.md#numbered-edge-case-inventory). | 2026-09-12T01:20:10+10:00 |
| M7-C56 | [x] Diagnose the consistently failing public admission gate. | verified local | w-admission worker, root validation | Resolved as two distinct defects, neither environmental; integrated as e1d3cef. Redis itself was fast throughout, with a median of 0.94 ms and a 99th percentile of 2.04 ms across 300 measured operations, so the two-second figures were the relay's own authority deadline firing on proxy-stalled commands rather than slow infrastructure. First, a fixture defect caused the gate failure: the Redis lookup-failure row injects a global authority outage that stalls every relay's connections, and it must hold that pause until the relay's own two-second authority deadline elapses or no typed lookup failure exists, a window measured at 2009.8 ms; because the maintenance tick runs every 500 ms, ticks always land inside that window and correctly close their sessions as authority-unavailable, so the row was destroying the very sessions later rows asserted were alive, nondeterministically according to which commands were in flight. That explains both observed symptoms and why load made it consistent. The row now runs after every row that needs a live pre-existing session, with no assertion weakened. Second, a real product defect surfaced during the investigation: the timestamped catalog scripts bounded caller-to-authority clock disagreement symmetrically at one second, but a script only sees the timestamp the caller sampled before dispatch, so that quantity is skew plus in-flight latency; with a two-second authority deadline, every reply landing between one and two seconds was deterministically refused as clock skew and surfaced as an authority conflict, killing healthy sessions whose leases were still renewing (reproduced at 1858 ms on a device resolution). The bound is now split by direction: a caller ahead of the authority stays capped at one second because that is genuine skew where extending a validity window would be unsafe, while a caller lagging is bounded by the authority deadline, which stays fail-closed because validity is evaluated at the later of the two timestamps and windows are translated back into the caller's frame, so a lagging timestamp can only shorten a window. Red-then-green: a caller lagging 1.5 seconds inside the deadline was refused as a conflict and is now accepted, while a caller 1.5 seconds ahead is still refused and the existing clock-bound expiry test passes unchanged. The gate passed six consecutive times across the fix, and the Redis partition and fail-closed admission gates pass. **Edge-case tests:** [FP-01, FP-03, EC-003, EC-011, EC-013, EC-023, EC-048, IN-12](m7-edge-cases.md#failure-policy-summary-rows). | 2026-09-12T01:28:40+10:00 |
| M7-C57 | [x] Retry the owner's bounded not-ready outcome in the peer-readiness recovery loop. | verified local | w-readiness worker, root validation | Integrated as 406f098. After the readiness convergence fix the peer-readiness gate reported `unexpected HTTP status 503` on its recovery probe while every relay snapshot was healthy. The real cause is a harness classification gap: route readiness recovers before the selected owner finishes re-establishing its device session, and in that window the relay returns its documented bounded retry for an incomplete owner carrier or fence, which is retryable with a 250 ms hint, but the recovery loop retried only the partition and cluster-unready codes, so a correct typed outcome was reported as unexpected. Verified by three consecutive passes of `verify-m7-peer-readiness` at 406f098 (15021, 15241 and 15259 ms, `recovery_echo=true` each) together with the peer-capacity and production gates. The supervisor publishes the terminal status, then publishes the typed readiness reason, then returns, which drops both watch senders in the same poll; the select therefore had two ready branches and chose at random, and the status branch mapped its sender-dropped error to a generic supervisor failure while the readiness channel was still holding the typed cause. The fix rests on a property of watch channels: a dropped sender does not erase the last value, so both stopped-channel paths now consult the retained readiness first and report the typed closed error, and the generic supervisor failure remains only when no terminal reason was ever published, so the fix cannot invent a cause. Two deterministic regressions drop the publisher before the terminal is classified and assert the typed cause survives through both the joined and reaped paths while a non-terminal readiness still yields the generic failure. Design review 2026-09-12 corrected this row's own framing: a forward sequence bound anchored on the quiesce snapshot would reject frames that are legitimate today, because a connector flushes frames queued before its freeze and they are valid up to the fence it is about to attest. The workable shape is retroactive: record the per-stream maximum admitted receive sequence for the quiesce roster while quiescing, then compare it against the attested fence when FROZEN arrives and fail closed with `FENCE_VIOLATION` if any admitted sequence exceeds it. The check is retroactive rather than the forward bound this row originally described, and that distinction is the design: before FROZEN there is no fence to compare against, and a connector legitimately flushes frames queued before its own freeze which stay valid up to the fence it is about to attest, so a bound derived from the relay's cursor at quiesce would reject exactly the in-flight frames the drain exists to receive. | M7-C71 | [x] Flush a session's terminal FIN toward the device before relay shutdown cancels the device listener. | verified local | w-c71 worker, root validation | Integrated as 52b0cb3, and the investigation found two distinct causes rather than the one the row recorded. First, `RunningRelay::shutdown` cancelled the shared token before asking the actor to shut down, so the device listener drained while the FIN could still be queued; the cancel now runs after the actor shutdown request on all three arms, including the expired-deadline arm, so a non-answering actor still cannot hang shutdown and `Drop` remains the emergency path. Second, and the one the regression actually proves, `close_all` cancelled and then discarded every queued command, so a close arriving in that window never produced a FIN at all; the drain now applies a deliberately narrow allowlist of two synchronous terminal commands and drops everything else exactly as before, with no await, spawn or catalog call. Red-then-green: `shutdown_drain_flushes_a_racing_stream_fin_before_the_device_close` reserves two command permits and sends Shutdown and CloseEchoStream back to back so the race is exact rather than probabilistic, and with the product edit reverted the drained carrier shows only `["Close"]` with no FIN. Relay lib 303 tests pass. Two limits recorded deliberately: a unit test proves the FIN reaches the writer's queue, not that the device read it, so the FP-05 `raw_fin_frames` allowance is untouched; and the first cause is not separately covered, because `close_all` cancels the listener token itself as its first action, which shrinks the window rather than closing it, and reordering that cancel must not precede the receiver close that stops background senders. That residual is the next thing to look at and is a genuine reason not to tighten the gate on this evidence. The worker also declined to add the writer barrier this row suggested: against a device socket that is already gone nothing answers it, so each session would pay a timeout out of the cleanup budget and a lost FIN would become a slow shutdown, which is the worse failure. **Edge-case tests:** [FP-05, EC-035](m7-edge-cases.md#numbered-edge-case-inventory). | 2026-09-13T20:40:00+10:00 |

Design review 2026-09-12 sharpened three of the seven: the EC-023 clause describes a reselection the relay deliberately does not implement, so it needs the row renegotiated rather than a test (see the EC-023 row); the EC-061 ordering clause cannot be observed from outside until the unregister is stamped from the same diagnostic clock as the fault tuple, because the two are microseconds apart with nothing marking the second; and the EC-041 control-attachment race has no barrier between device HELLO and WELCOME/101, so a deterministic proof needs a fixture-only `ControlAttachBarrier` mirroring the existing consumer upgrade barrier. The remaining four need no product change, and the recommended order is OG-02 (add the three non-peer-fault gates and make the per-row completeness rule not require a peer-fault tuple), then IN-05 (assert the `pool_connect`, `lease` and `owner` stages in gates that already induce those faults), then EC-025's third arm (hold the peer admission barrier at the exact owner scope and withdraw peer trust while it is held), then EC-044 (port the fragmentation fixture onto the real peer ingress handler). **Progress 2026-09-14:** the OG-02 clause is closed (5a2018e, matrix row promoted), leaving EC-061 ordering, IN-05 stages, EC-044 peer-path injection, EC-023 reselection, EC-025 peer-trust crossing and EC-041 control race. Of those, EC-023 needs the row renegotiated rather than a test, and EC-061's ordering half and EC-041's control half need product or fixture changes that do not exist yet. EC-025 closed 2026-09-14 (33eee6b), leaving EC-061 ordering, IN-05 stages, EC-044 peer-path injection, EC-023 reselection and EC-041 control race. The three clauses left are not test work: EC-061's ordering half needs the unregister stamped from the same diagnostic clock as the fault tuple, EC-041's control half needs a control-attach barrier that does not exist, and EC-023 asks for a bounded reselection the relay deliberately does not implement and needs the row renegotiated rather than a gate. **EC-061 closed 2026-09-14** across both halves: the ordering half stamps the owner unregister from the same diagnostic clock as the fault tuples (803520b), and the closure half (d9c8830) gives the control, data and consumer adapter tasks their own bounded closure stage and cause. The closure vocabulary is deliberately separate from the peer-fault vocabulary, which names peer ingress and owner-side failures: reusing it for an ordinary socket close would have inflated fault counters and per-stage latches with routine closes. It shares the same mutex, sequence and clock, which is what makes a closure and an unregister orderable against each other without being conflated. The cause variable carries no default, so the compiler proves every exit assigns one rather than a comment promising it. Four regressions drive the real exit of each task body and assert the closure's sequence precedes the unregister's, with a permanent negative control showing the predicate can fail. What they do not prove is recorded in the file: cause selection inside each socket loop needs a real mTLS device socket, and stamp placement relative to the removal cannot be observed single-threaded. **Two clauses remain.** EC-041's control half is implemented and under repair, not done: the `ControlAttachBarrier` exists and the held HELLO-to-WELCOME race runs, but the new phase leaves the pre-existing later scenario unable to reach a live owner, so the gate fails at its consumer handshake with 503. | M7-C76 | [x] Close the chaos, parity and guard residuals the second audit named. | verified local | root | OG-08: measure reconnects at second scale and attribute them to the CLI (exclude the fixture's recycle sockets from `device_fanout.accepted`), repeat Redis chaos with observed post-pause recovery instead of a single terminal round, classify a CLI exiting before readiness instead of surfacing it as a harness error, and add margin under the unknown-outcome ceiling. Closed: the reconnect rate is now the largest count of client-attributed accepts in a real one-second window rather than a whole-round average, which previously let a ten-reconnect burst inside a ten-second round read as one per second and pass a twelve-per-second threshold; attribution confirms the audit's suspicion, since 12 of the 14 accepts per run are the harness recycling its own sockets and only 2 are the client. Also closed: a CLI exiting before readiness is a typed class carrying its exit code and the client's own diagnostic code, with an untyped code blocking release. **Still open, and the recorded reason for it was wrong:** repeated Redis chaos with observed recovery. The membership lease is not irrecoverable as previously documented; all three relays return to Ready on their own in about 28 seconds, `membership_runtime.rs:1088` never exits on error and `:1578` is the sole unconditional Ready with no latch. The real obstacle is that the fixture's own membership record lifetime is 60 seconds with a 20-second refresh (`production_cluster.rs:4808`), so there is no headroom by the terminal round, and closing this needs that fixture change rather than a schedule change. The re-arm observation is recorded as evidence and deliberately never asserted, because asserting it would make the gate pass by accident in one schedule position and fail in another. Four fixture candidates were ruled out by experiment rather than assumption (fanout target schedule and wrap, fanout saturation, peer-pin republication, relay liveness endpoints); why reuse still fails once membership re-arms was isolated to the device control handshake but not reached, because the client sanitizes the handshake detail and the relay logs nothing at refusal. The unknown-outcome ceiling still has no margin, at 2 of 2, untouched by this work. **Progress 2026-09-14 (integrated as 59ce4e6 and 282fc0f).** The third chaos clause is closed and the earlier recorded reason for it was itself wrong twice over. Repeated Redis chaos with observed recovery now holds: the schedule runs two full Redis pauses mid-schedule with further rounds after each, every Redis round asserts that all three relays return to Ready, and the owner session is recycled and echoed on afterwards, so recovery is proved at the session level and cluster reuse is proved by the rounds that follow. The fixture change this needed was not a longer record. A first attempt signed the record for 900 seconds and the gate then failed in four seconds, four runs running, at `relay relay-a membership bootstrap did not reach Ready`: the relay's verifier caps a record at the product maximum of 60 seconds and refuses anything longer outright, so a longer record is not purchasable at any price. The fixture instead keeps issuing records on a 15-second interval inside that cap, which is what a control plane does and which widens nothing the relay accepts. Re-signing is opt-in per scenario, because a background version bump would collide with the trust-expiry, key-overlap and handover gates that publish their own records at chosen versions. The re-signer rides out a deliberate Redis pause rather than dying inside the gate that paused it, gives up only after failures spanning longer than one record lifetime, and its first error is surfaced at shutdown rather than logged and forgotten. **The remaining clause is unchanged and is stated plainly rather than implied away:** the unknown-outcome ceiling still has no margin, at 2 of 2, and that is now known to be deterministic rather than incidental. Both peer-loss rounds produce a typed `PEER_UNAVAILABLE` with `execution=unknown`, which is the relay's correct answer when the transport fails after a request may have reached the owner, so the peer-loss fault never yields the typed peer-unavailable class in a live run and any third unknown fails the gate. The claim is sharpened instead: every preserved unknown must be attributable to a peer-loss round, which is the one fault here whose outcome is legitimately indeterminate, since the relay forwards toward the owner, loses the path, and cannot know whether the request was dispatched. An unknown arising from any other fault is now rejected even at a count the ceiling would accept, and a run claiming more peer-loss unknowns than it had peer-loss rounds is rejected too. That also explains the 2 of 2 the audit flagged: it is deterministic, it is the two peer-loss rounds, and the evidence line says so. Six consecutive runs report both unknowns attributed to peer loss, with two red controls isolating each new rule. IN-10/OG-05 closed with M7-C74 and the heartbeat, liveness and measured shutdown join assertions; both matrix rows are promoted. | M7-C77 | [x] Complete a post-GOAWAY peer drain when the peer closes with QUIC application code 0. | verified local | root | Root cause of the I08 GOAWAY gate's cluster-cleanup failures in two consecutive full surveys (different relays each time, the scenario itself passing). After a remote GOAWAY the peer client driver drains and classifies the connection's terminal state with `planned_idle_result`, which accepted only h3's `H3_NO_ERROR` (0x100); every graceful close in `tunnel-transport` and the relay closes its QUIC connection and endpoint with application code 0, which carries no HTTP/3 error at all. Because a cluster stops its relays in sequence, a later relay was still draining a pooled connection to one already stopped, read that clean close as a protocol failure, and reported `stopping relay <node>: peer transport failed`. Two corroborations before changing anything: a reproduction under `tunnel_transport=debug` logged exactly `ApplicationClose: 0x0` at the failing teardown, and the existing GOAWAY fixture already closes with `H3_NO_ERROR` carrying a comment that calls application code 0 ambiguous, working around the behaviour rather than exposing it. Fixed in 7c28ce6: the drain also accepts a remote application close carrying code 0, through a narrow vendored h3 accessor (`is_remote_no_error_application_close`; the `Remote` variant is otherwise reachable only behind the third-party-backend feature, and the patch is recorded in `vendor/h3/UPSTREAM_PATCH.md`). Every real protocol failure arrives with a defined 0x1xx code and is still reported. Evidence: the gate passes 25 of 25 consecutive runs after the fix against an observed pre-fix failure rate near one in eight (probability of that run of successes at the old rate, 0.035). Honest limit on the unit coverage: the two new `m7_peer_goaway` cases pin the invariant with a production-shaped close but pass before the fix as well, because with no stream lease outstanding the driver can finish its drain without reaching the classifier; they are labelled characterization, not red-then-green, and the repeated gate runs are the evidence. A broader change that made shutdown ignore every terminal error was written and then reverted, because with the specific cause known it would have suppressed genuine protocol errors. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-039, EC-042, IN-02](m7-edge-cases.md#numbered-edge-case-inventory). | 2026-09-12T11:26:00+10:00 |
| M7-C78 | [x] Report peer connection-pool exhaustion as typed capacity instead of a timeout. | verified local | root | Root cause of `verify-m7-peer-capacity` failing roughly one run in six **on an idle machine**, so not load. `PeerClient::connect` waited on the connection semaphore under the handshake deadline; `with_checkout_deadline` maps deadline expiry to `Timeout` and the `?` propagated that before the `map_err` intended to produce `Capacity`, which only ever saw the semaphore's own closed error. An exhausted pool therefore reported a generic timeout after the full ten-second budget and never reported capacity at all, so `refresh_required_routes` recorded the route `Unreachable` rather than `CapacityExhausted`, collapsing the distinction the peer readiness model is built on. Instrumentation made it unambiguous: every probe failure measured 5001 ms, the probe's entire budget, racing the gate's equally sized window. Fixed in `tunnel-transport`: take a free permit immediately, otherwise wait a bounded `CAPACITY_RELEASE_GRACE` (250 ms) and report typed capacity either way. Red-then-green by isolation: with the change removed, `exhausted_connection_pool_is_typed_capacity_not_a_timeout` fails in 10.01 s reporting a timeout instead of capacity. Recorded process note, because the first attempt was wrong: refusing instantly with no grace also broke ordinary connection replacement, where the branch above removes an unusable pooled connection whose permit lands only when the last handle drops, and measurement showed the gate get worse (five failures in fourteen against the one-in-six baseline) before the grace corrected it to thirteen in fourteen. The residual was a second, independent cause in the gate rather than the product: `CAPACITY_COMPARISON_WINDOW` equalled the relay's own `PEER_PROBE_TIMEOUT`, so the two deadlines expired together and the gate could fail while the product behaved correctly; the window is now 15 s and the assertion is unchanged, still requiring typed capacity and still failing a generic timeout. Final measurement: 16 of 16 consecutive passes (probability of that at the one-in-six baseline, 0.054). **Edge-case tests:** [IN-01, EC-034, EC-051](m7-edge-cases.md#numbered-edge-case-inventory). | 2026-09-12T16:18:00+10:00 |

The bundle reported a failing child as counts and markers only, and its capture directory is a `TempDir` removed when the case ends, so each intermittent failure took its own diagnosis with it; `run_case` now preserves the child's streams when `C11_CHILD_FAILURE_DIR` names a directory and puts the path in the error, which carries a path rather than any child content. That caught a failure on the fifth run. The preserved stderr showed the credential-expiry gate failing inside its pre-rotation keepalive loop while both CLIs were healthy (`state=running`, `phase=active`, sessions present, `error=missing`, `protocol_causes=[]`): the loop bounded `round_trip_all` by the minimum of three values, one being the time until the arm point, which collapses to nothing as the loop approaches it, so an iteration landing a few milliseconds short gave a real two-tenant round trip an impossible window. Inside the bundle, slower snapshot polling makes that landing point more variable, which is why it appeared there and not standalone. The loop now waits out the remainder rather than starting a round trip it would have to fail.
