| M7-C80 | [ ] Keep in-flight peer streams across a same-key membership re-sign at a higher record version. | planned | unassigned (defect found by the m3c4 worker) | **Defect.** A newer signed membership record version for an unchanged node, boot and key invalidates every peer admission bound to the old version (`membership_runtime.rs`, the `current_version != Some(peer.record_version)` invalidation). Every in-flight peer stream is invalidated with it. For `http-forward/1` a long-lived SSE response through a non-owner ingress therefore dies at each membership refresh: the ingress sees `HTTP_STREAM_INTERRUPTED` and the owner `RESET(CANCELLED)`, observed in `verify-m3-http-forward-rotation` before that gate aligned re-signs to case boundaries. It is recorded as a known interruption in [cluster.md](cluster.md).<br><br>**Remaining.** Decide whether a same-key, same-binding version bump with a non-shrinking trust deadline may keep existing admissions (re-binding them to the new version), or keep the interruption and bound its frequency. Then prove it with a long-lived peer stream across a re-sign.<br><br>**Not the owner of the `verify-m3-mcp-isolation` flake (established 2026-09-16, M7-C83).** That symptom was read here as "an in-flight peer stream dying at a re-sign". Owner-side correlation shows it was not: the ingress recorded a single `pool_connect` fault, **no relay recorded an owner-role tuple**, so no stream was in flight and the owner never saw the request. It was a fresh dial refused on an empty pin set, fixed under M7-C83. This row's own scope - an admission, and any stream already riding it, invalidated by a same-key version bump - is untouched by that fix and stays open. | — |
| M7-C81 | [ ] Recover peer readiness after membership re-signs in quick succession. | planned | unassigned (defect found by the m3c4 worker) | **Defect.** After three case-boundary re-signs (versions 2, 3 and 4 published about 0.35 s and 6.5 s apart), every relay's membership runtime stayed `Ready` with all records at version 4, but `PeerRuntime::is_ready()` stayed false on all three relays. Public requests got `503 CLUSTER_UNREADY` for the 45 s route check, and readiness had still not recovered 120 s later. All 120 `production peer readiness probe failed` warnings in that window were `Transport(H3("Connection error: Remote error: ApplicationClose: 0x0"))`. That points to probes reusing a pooled HTTP/3 connection the remote closed on admission invalidation, rather than dialling a new one.<br><br>**Reproduction** (1 of 3 runs failed this way; the other two passed all selected cases): `M3_ROTATION_RESIGN_SPACING_MS=0 M3_ROTATION_CASES=admission-probe,cancel-race,head,partial-header,partial-body TEST_REDIS_URL=redis://127.0.0.1:63790/ TUNNEL_CATALOG_REDIS_URL=redis://127.0.0.1:63790/ cargo run --locked -p tunnel-test-harness -- verify-m3-http-forward-rotation`. Branch `m3c4-rotation`, commit 91538ed. A partial case list always fails validation, so look only for the `CLUSTER_UNREADY` route-check error. It was not fixed here; the gate re-signs at most every 15 s.<br><br>**Root cause, found 2026-09-16 by the m3c6 worker:** not a pooled connection. The production-cluster fixture publishes peer pins from the membership invalidation callback only. A re-sign can invalidate an admission while the runtime is momentarily not `Ready`; that publication fails closed, replaces the pin set with an empty one, and nothing republishes it, so every relay withdraws peer trust for good and each fresh probe is closed by the remote with QUIC application code 0. The serving relay (`tunnel-relay/src/main.rs`) republishes pins on every refresh tick and does not have this behaviour, so this is a fixture divergence, not a product defect. **Fixed in the fixture** on branch `m3c6-mcp-cloud-client`: a failed publication is remembered and retried by the refresh loop once the runtime is `Ready` again, while an explicit withdrawal (the key-revocation fixture's) is still held. Red-then-green: before the fix, `verify-m3-mcp-cloud-client` failed at its first or second case-boundary re-sign in three of five runs with 45 s of `503 CLUSTER_UNREADY` and 90 `ApplicationClose: 0x0` probe failures; after it, nine consecutive runs re-signed four to six times each with none. **Re-run of the original reproduction** (`M3_ROTATION_RESIGN_SPACING_MS=0 M3_ROTATION_CASES=admission-probe,cancel-race,head,partial-header,partial-body … verify-m3-http-forward-rotation`) against the fixed fixture, three times: each run published four back-to-back re-signs, hit the failing-closed pin publication four times, and recovered every time — zero `readiness probe failed` warnings, zero `CLUSTER_UNREADY` answers, all five selected cases reached. Each run then failed only on the gate's own rule that a shortened re-sign spacing is not gate evidence, as designed. **Remaining** work moved to M7-C83. | — |
| M7-C82 | [ ] Release an OPEN journal entry at the owner's retry horizon so a session is not limited to 128 streams. | implemented awaiting verification | m3c7 worker (defect found by the m3c6 worker) | **Defect.** The connector's OPEN journal kept one entry per admitted stream for the session's lifetime (active entries plus tombstones, capped at 128 by `MAX_JOURNAL_ENTRIES`), and a tombstone was never released. A session therefore admitted at most 128 streams in its whole lifetime, which under `mcp-2026-07-28` is 128 MCP requests. The documented exhaustion outcome existed (`RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`, "start a fresh session"), but nothing started a fresh session and the next owner-ordered `STREAM_FORGET` for that refused, unjournaled stream was rejected as `STREAM_FORGET unknown stream or OPEN journal entry`: the connector failed the session, the owner closed it `CONTROL_CLOSED`, and the device stopped.<br><br>**Protocol rule first.** [protocol.md](protocol.md#open-retry-horizon-and-journal-reclamation) now states the missing rule normatively: the owner may retry an `OPEN` message ID only while the connector still retains its entry, and the owner's own authenticated `STREAM_FORGET` for that stream and operation is the horizon, because issuing it asserts final cursor/terminal evidence, no drain or replay reference and exclusion from every later snapshot. An owner that could still retry has not finished with the stream and must not have sent the message. Epoch change, owner change and session loss end the window with the session that scopes the journal. Testable invariant 19 was added.<br><br>**Implemented.** The connector releases the canonical request, retained reply and tombstone once a `STREAM_FORGET` completes its carrier barriers (`OpenJournal::release_matching`), so the journal is bounded by unreclaimed entries rather than by streams served. An authenticated `STREAM_FORGET` is benign when it names a reclaimed stream, one refused before it could be journaled, or any ID at or below the monotonic `forgotten_stream_through` watermark; one naming an unretained ID above the watermark stays a protocol error. The watermark is a benign condition in its own right because the owner allocates stream IDs monotonically and can consume one without ever naming it in an `OPEN` (a failed control encode, or a full control queue), so an ID below it was allocated by this owner in this session. `RetiredStreamIds` (disjoint ranges) covers the second case only, since an ID refused before journaling can sit above the watermark; its range count is bounded by coalescing the lowest gap rather than by an invariant, because such never-named IDs open gaps that no retained entry accounts for — coalescing is counted, absorbs only IDs already below the watermark, and never makes an ID admissible. Idempotency is unchanged: an `OPEN` naming a stream ID at or below the watermark for which the session holds nothing is refused `STREAM_EXISTS` before admission **and without journaling the refusal** (journaling it would retain an entry the owner can never forget again, so a rule-violating owner could leak one entry per refusal until the session wedged at `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`), so a retry past the horizon is never dispatched twice and no result is fabricated; the connector can no longer tell such a retry from a reused message ID with different contents, and refuses both. **Known limitation, pre-existing and unchanged:** because the predicate is a watermark, an `OPEN` still waiting in the bounded admission queue when a later stream ID is forgotten is refused `STREAM_EXISTS` if it reaches admission afterwards, though it was never dispatched; the owner sees a typed refusal and no dispatch. Real exhaustion of concurrently live entries keeps its `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED` outcome. `ConnectionStatus` gained payload-free `open_journal_entries`, `open_streams_retired` and `open_retired_ranges_coalesced`.<br><br>**Evidence.** Unit and property tests in `m2_runtime.rs`: 512 sequential open/forget cycles keep the journal at one entry at a time and one retired range; a retry for a live entry still replays its exact retained `OPENED`; a retry past the horizon is refused `STREAM_EXISTS` and leaves the journal empty; 512 repeated post-horizon retries cannot fill the journal and a fresh stream ID is still admitted afterwards; a `STREAM_FORGET` for a reclaimed, never-journaled or below-watermark stream is benign but only after it authenticates (a stale epoch or another session's ID is a context mismatch); one for an unretained ID above the watermark still fails; retired-ID membership is exact against a `BTreeSet` reference over 4,288 inserts and coalesces only past its bound. `verify-m3-http-forward-real-path` gained a sequential phase: 160 small requests on one device session, highest device stream ID 164, 160 dispatches, 0 typed retries, journal peak **1** entry, reclaimed IDs rising by exactly 160 (3 → 163), 0 coalesced gaps, session still ready and `active`. Of the four stream IDs allocated before the loop, three were reclaimed: the one ID the run never reclaims is from that earlier phase, by elimination the `/events` exchange the consumer cancelled by disconnecting, which is the run's only aborted exchange. Red-then-green at three levels: with the release reverted the unit test fails at stream 129 and the gate dies at sequential request 124 with the exact readiness `Closed { reason: "STREAM_FORGET unknown stream or OPEN journal entry" }` and a journal peak of 128; with the unjournaled refusal reverted the horizon tests fail with one leaked entry each; with the pre-benignity authentication reverted a stale-epoch `STREAM_FORGET` for a retired ID is silently accepted.<br><br>**Blocking marker dropped.** The product no longer dies after 128 streams and a gate proves it, so the one-session-per-combination arrangement in `verify-m3-mcp-cloud-client` is no longer a workaround for a product ceiling; [mcp.md](mcp.md) and [testing.md](testing.md) now say it is that gate's own scoping choice, and its `highest_call_stream_id < 128` rule is retained as an invariant of that scoping rather than relaxed, because relaxing it inside a gate that still uses one session per combination would prove nothing. This row is itself unverified, so it must be verified before an M3 row that depends on the removed ceiling is.<br><br>**Reproduction re-run green.** The row's own reproduction was re-run once against the fixed connector: `verify-m3-mcp-cloud-client` with `connect_device` temporarily hoisted out of the combination loop and its `highest_call_stream_id < 128` and per-combination child rules temporarily relaxed. One device session served all four combinations and all 28 cases across 51 rotations, reaching call stream ID 141 with `session_stable` true throughout and `device_sessions: 1`; the run then failed only on that gate's own bookkeeping rule that there is one session per combination. Pre-fix the same arrangement died at about 130 streams. The patch was reverted and is not part of the gate. The reviewer of this fix found the narrower M7-C85, a consumer-cancelled exchange whose journal entry the real-path gate never sees reclaimed; this fix does not address it. **Not proven.** This row has not been re-verified at a merged revision. It has had independent review - that reviewer found the narrower M7-C85 - so the earlier "no independent review evidence" note is superseded. At `db1da30` the sequential phase of `verify-m3-http-forward-real-path` that exercises this reclamation passed 7 of 7 runs (journal peak 1 entry, highest device stream ID 164); that corroborates the fix without verifying this row's own scope. | — |
| M7-C83 | [ ] Regress back-to-back membership re-signs, and decide whether the serving relay's own pin callback should retry. | planned | mcp (split out of M7-C81) | **Remaining after the M7-C81 fixture fix.** Two pieces. (1) A focused regression that re-signs membership back to back and requires peer readiness to recover, rather than relying on the M3-08 rotation gate's shortened-spacing reproduction, which is deliberately not gate evidence. (2) A decision for the serving relay (`tunnel-relay/src/main.rs`): it republishes pins on every refresh tick, so it recovers from the same failed-closed publication, but its invalidation callback has no retry of its own; either document the tick as the recovery path or give the callback the same pending-retry the fixture now has. **Reproduced on 2026-09-16** by the M3 verification pass: `verify-m3-mcp-isolation` failed 2 of 17 runs at `db1da30` and 2 of 10 after that gate gained a timeout diagnostic, each time with the consumer seeing `503 PEER_UNAVAILABLE` `execution=unknown` for a request issued just after a case-boundary re-sign, and each failing run carrying exactly one failed-closed pin publication and no readiness-probe failure. **Hypothesis, not established:** the re-sign's failed-closed publication empties the transport pin set, and the request then either rides a peer hop closed under it or fails a fresh dial against the empty set.  The consumer's status is the same transport-failure arm for both, so it does not separate them; the roughly 30 s the POST stayed outstanding before the 503 fits a handshake or idle timeout better than an immediate pin-watcher close. The consumer's status cannot distinguish a request lost before the owner from one the owner received, so **the gate must also capture owner-side correlation for that exchange before the cause is settled**, and M7-C80 may be the closer owner, its symptom being an in-flight peer stream dying at a re-sign. **Part 2 applies to the product, not only the fixture:** `tunnel-relay/src/main.rs`'s invalidation callback clears pins on a failed-closed publication and recovers only on the next refresh tick, so the serving relay has the same window; whether that callback should retry is this row's open question.<br><br>**(On branch `m3c10-isolation-flake`, held as M7-C86; not on `main` and not on the gate-only branch.)** **Part 2 answered and the hypothesis settled on 2026-09-16 (commits `1fb2d99` and `925cf9c`).** Owner-side correlation was added to the gate and three reproductions agree: the ingress recorded exactly one `ingress/pool_connect/transport_other` tuple, **no relay recorded an owner-role tuple**, and the consumer was answered in 12-14 ms. So it is the **fresh dial refused on an empty pin set**, not a hop closed mid-request, and the "roughly 30 s outstanding" reading is withdrawn as an artefact of the gate's own marker wait. The upstream trigger is now named by a new reconcile warning: a re-sign races the reconcile, `install_unready_candidate` sets `Unready(MembershipRejected)` and invalidates every admission, and the callback's publication then fails closed. No readiness probe fails because the refresh tick republishes before it probes. **The answer to part 2 is that the callback needs no retry, because a *locally* unready runtime should never have withdrawn peer trust in the first place:** readiness is about this relay's own prerequisites, and `peer_refresh_loop`'s unready branch already documented keeping the verified pin set installed and withdrawing readiness instead, but could not reach that branch because `publish_membership_pins` had already emptied the set. That is fixed in the product and mirrored in the fixture, and **narrowed on a recorded owner decision after review**: rejected trust evidence (unverifiable authority, failed record verification, expired checkpoint) still withdraws the pin set, while a local or transient unready state retains it. The first, unnarrowed attempt broke `m7_deployment_spki_replacement` at phase 6, where a rogue-signed record must make relay A refuse both certificates on the probe; that gate is green under the split and the regression was reproduced and re-fixed in both directions. The bounded refresh tick remains the documented recovery path for a *genuine* publication failure, which still fails closed. A dial that does race an empty set now answers `not_dispatched` with a retry hint (`PeerTransportError::PinsUnavailable`, cause `transport_pins_unavailable`) rather than `unknown`. Evidence: `verify-m3-mcp-isolation` failed 6 of 24 runs before and 0 of 8 after, then 12 of 12 at `6297458` and 18 of 18 standalone in total since the fix.<br><br>**Remaining.** **Part 2 is still open on `main`:** the serving relay's invalidation callback still publishes an empty pin set on a failed-closed publication and recovers only on the next refresh tick, because the change that would alter that is held as M7-C86 and is on neither `main` nor the gate-only branch. Part 1: a focused regression that re-signs membership back to back and requires peer readiness to recover, rather than relying on the M3-08 rotation gate's shortened-spacing reproduction. The M3-04 gate is not that regression - it re-signs at case boundaries at most every 15 s and asserts MCP behaviour, not readiness recovery.<br><br>**And a new blocker this row now owns.** The retention split regresses `verify-m7-trust-expiry` intermittently: **0 of 4 at the branch base, about 1 in 3 on the branch**, failing with `expired target pooled stream was not observed with its exact owner session/cursor before reclamation` while every correctness field in the sample is true. Retaining pins through an unready window delays the transport pin watcher's closure of the pooled peer stream that gate observes before reclamation. Isolated by elimination: not the `MissingLocalKey` classification (both arms fail), not retaining a `Ready`-derived empty set (that variant was tried and reverted), not skipping `mark_route_unreachable` for `PinsUnavailable` (reverted variant still fails) - the core retention itself. **Diagnosed 2026-09-16; the closure hypothesis is refuted.** The proposal that the gate passes only because withdrawing the pin set closes the expired peer's pooled stream does not survive the evidence. Closure is already driven by the expiry: in **every** run, passing or failing, the owner records `cause=PeerMembershipExpired`, `stream_removed=true` and exactly one matching terminal event. Of the seventeen conditions the gate joins on one sample, exactly one ever differs - `ingress_last_receive`, which is `(IngressReceive, TrustExpired)` when it passes and `(IngressReceive, Closed)` when it fails. Making closure explicit would not move that field.<br><br>**What the pin layer is actually load-bearing for is the ingress's *attribution*, not the closure.** `TrustExpired` is `PeerRuntimeError::MembershipExpired`, produced by `attribute_admission_failure` (`peer_runtime.rs`), which reclassifies a peer-reset-family close only when `PeerAdmissionCancellation::trust_expired()` holds. That predicate is true when this edge's invalidation dispatcher has already recorded `TrustExpired`, or - as a fallback - when the admission's own monotonic deadline has passed. At the branch base the pin set is emptied **five times per run** (five `pin publication failed closed` warnings), so the ingress's edge is invalidated promptly and a `TrustExpired` reason is latched before the owner's close is observed. With the retention split those five become four retains and one genuine withdrawal, the ingress's dispatcher lags by up to a reconcile tick, and instrumentation at the decision point shows the close arriving with `reason=None`, `expires_at_set=true`, `trust_expired=false` - so neither arm fires and the close stays `Closed`. The two relays hold independent membership runtimes reconciling on independent ticks, so the ingress can legitimately lag the owner at the same signed boundary.<br><br>**Measured.** Base: 6 of 6 pass, every satisfying sample `TrustExpired`, five failed-closed publications per run. Branch: 3 of 6 pass, four retains plus one withdrawal per run, and the pin-warning counts are **identical between its passing and failing runs** - so pin state is not what separates them within the branch; the dispatcher race is.<br><br>**Proposed, not implemented, pending a decision.** The fallback arm is too weak for what its own docstring already promises: *"The peer relay enforces the same signed boundary and may reset or end a pooled stream first; the observer of that failure must still attribute it to the earlier trust deadline rather than to a generic transport close."* It compares only `AdmissionDeadline::expires_at`, this relay's monotonic admission deadline. `AdmissionDeadline` also carries `trust_expires_at`, the **signed** wall-clock boundary both relays share, and that is not plumbed into `PeerAdmissionCancellation`. Plumbing it and letting `trust_expired()` also fire on the signed boundary would make the attribution independent of which relay's dispatcher ran first - the same "explicit, not accidental" principle, applied at the site that actually decides the outcome. It changes no closure behaviour and touches one predicate plus one constructor. **Implemented on the owner's authorisation (2026-09-16, commit `c8ec3fb`), and it narrows the race without closing it.** `PeerAdmissionCancellation` now carries the signed wall-clock boundary alongside this relay's monotonic deadline, and `trust_expired()` fires on either, so attribution no longer depends on which dispatcher ran first or on the pin set's contents. The boundary is `VerifiedPeerBinding::valid_until` - the earlier of the record's and the authorising key's expiry - **not** `AdmissionDeadline::trust_expires_at`: instrumenting the decision point showed the record boundary still 46 s away at a failing attribution, because a key can leave its own signed window while the record carrying it stays valid, and it is the key window a peer enforces. `valid_until` is exactly the "earlier trust deadline" the docstring named. Red-then-green at the decision point: with only the monotonic fallback, `a_peer_reset_after_the_signed_boundary_is_expiry_before_the_local_deadline` fails on the observed shape; the same test pins that a reset *before* the boundary is not expiry and that an explicit different reason still wins, so the rule adds positive evidence and never invents it. The normative statement is in [cluster.md](cluster.md#implementation-and-acceptance-gates).<br><br>**Still open, and not averaged away.** `verify-m7-trust-expiry` improves from 3 of 6 to **14 of 18** across identical code (8 of 10, then 6 of 8 at `c8ec3fb`), against **6 of 6 at the branch base**. It is not 8 of 8, so the attribution is still racing. Every remaining failure has the same shape as before - sixteen of seventeen conditions hold, only `ingress_last_receive` is `Closed` - and the captured reason is that the reset arrives **before any signed boundary has passed**. If that observation is right, the stream is being reset for something that is not expiry at that instant, and forcing those cases to `TrustExpired` would invent the evidence this rule forbids; the open question is then why the owner resets early in roughly a fifth of runs, and whether the gate's assertion holds for that ordering at all. That needs an owner decision, and nothing further was written.<br><br>**Base instrumented on 2026-09-16 to test whether the gate's assertion is itself wrong. Answer: no, and two premises this work rested on are corrected.** The question was whether base ever attributes `TrustExpired` to a reset preceding every signed boundary — which would mean the pin blackout was standing in as evidence and the gate had been asserting an outcome the contract forbids. Twelve instrumented runs at `19ba287`, recording each reset's arrival against `VerifiedPeerBinding::valid_until`, the record/checkpoint deadline and the monotonic deadline: **base does not invent evidence.** Eleven of the twelve passing runs never reach the reclassification path at all — the ingress's own dispatcher latches `PeerInvalidationReason::TrustExpired` and `admission_cancellation_error` raises `MembershipExpired` directly, which is positive evidence by definition. The single reclassification caught (`base2-11`) fired on `monotonic_passed=true` with the record boundary 74 ms ahead: the monotonic conversion of that same signed boundary, i.e. clock skew on one boundary, not a pre-boundary attribution. The 24 `AsIs` samples — exactly two per run, in every run — sit ~49 s before their boundary and are correctly *not* attributed; they belong to a different edge, so the earlier reading that "the owner resets before expiry in a fifth of runs" was a misattribution of those sibling resets and is withdrawn.<br><br>**Correction 1: `binding.valid_until` is the wrong boundary.** The same samples show `record_until` (the trust/checkpoint window, already represented monotonically by `AdmissionDeadline::expires_at`) *earlier* than `valid_until`: 11:42:26.741 against 11:43:21.399. The governing signed boundary is therefore the one the existing monotonic arm already carries, and the boundary added in `c8ec3fb` can only fire **later** than it — so it adds nothing in the window that matters. That change is not harmful (it only adds positive evidence after a later boundary) but it does not address the failure, and its apparent effect should not be read as one.<br><br>**Correction 2: the base is not 6 of 6.** Instrumented, base is **11 of 12** — it flakes at roughly 8%. The branch's 14 of 18 is therefore a smaller regression than the earlier 6-of-6-against-3-of-6 comparison implied, and part of the difference between rounds is machine load rather than code. Both numbers should be re-measured against each other on a quiet machine before any further conclusion is drawn.<br><br>**The missed-latch hypothesis is refuted too, by instrumenting the dispatcher (2026-09-16).** `dispatch_invalidations` was instrumented identically on both revisions to log every `(node, peer, reason, time)`. Across four interleaved runs — base and branch, passing and failing — the traces are **identical**: exactly one `TrustExpired` per direction on the affected `relay-a`/`relay-c` edge, plus `RuntimeCancelled` at teardown, in all four. The ingress's dispatcher therefore latches `TrustExpired` in every run, including the failing ones, so the retention split neither delays nor drops an invalidation the dispatcher needs. That was this row's stated next question and the answer is no.<br><br>**Two further facts from the same measurement.** First, **the race pre-exists the branch**: under the dispatcher instrumentation, which slows both arms, *base* failed too (`base-2`, gate unsatisfied) with the same identical dispatch trace. Second, uninstrumented and interleaved on the same machine, the branch still loses more often — see the measurement below — so the branch does make a pre-existing race more likely without changing what the dispatcher does.<br><br>**Quiet-machine measurement, interleaved.** Ten pairs, alternating base and branch, both binaries pre-built so no compilation ran during the measurement, with the load average recorded before every run (8.6-13.8 throughout on ten cores; the machine was **not** idle — an unrelated process held one core at 99% and was left alone — which is exactly why the arms are interleaved rather than compared across sessions). **Base 10 of 10; branch 6 of 10.** This supersedes both earlier numbers: the branch base is not the 6 of 6 first reported, nor is it only the 11 of 12 measured under instrumentation, and the branch's 14 of 18 was measured under different load. Two honest numbers, same conditions: 10/10 against 6/10.<br><br>**What remains, stated as an unconfirmed hypothesis rather than a finding.** The dispatch is identical, so what differs must be the *ordering* between that dispatch and the ingress observing the peer's reset. At base the pin withdrawal closes the pooled connection **locally**, in the same invalidation that latches the reason, which serialises the local close after the local latch; with the set retained, the stream instead ends on a reset that crosses from the other relay and can arrive before the local latch. That would make the retention split a genuine cause of the increased failure rate without it being a dispatcher defect at all — but it is **not established**, it needs an experiment correlating the reset's arrival with the latch on the same timeline, and on this row's record every hypothesis acted on without that correlation has needed withdrawing. **No fix was written. M3-04 is still not promotable, and its remaining blocker is now M7-C86**, which holds the retention work unlanded with its cost, its measurements and the three refuted hypotheses. The parts of this row's work that do not touch peer-trust retention — the two new gate cases, the SSE reader fix, the typed `PinsUnavailable` answer and the owner-side correlation instrumentation — are landed separately and independently of it. | — |
| M7-C84 | [ ] Report an orderly connector stop as stopped, not as a transport failure. | planned | unassigned (defect found by the m3c6 worker) | **Defect.** Does not block M3-03: nothing is delivered or replayed, and the process was stopping anyway. `ConnectionHandle::stop` cancels the shared token; the M2 actor loop selects on cancellation first, but its deadline-tick body awaits `flush_pending_outputs`, `retry_all_http_parked` and `retry_pending_retire` before `retry_pending_forget_barriers`. The carrier writer task holds the same token and exits on cancellation, dropping its `CarrierCommand` receiver, so a stop landing inside that tick body resumes into a closed channel and ends the session with `Transport { scope: "stream forget barrier", detail: "data writer stopped before barrier completion" }`. The supervisor then fails the stop, so a user-requested stop returns `Err` and `Readiness::Closed` carries a fault reason. It needs only a pending `STREAM_FORGET` barrier; a rotation attempt merely widens the window, and the same latent flake affects every gate that treats a stop error as fatal (`http_forward_rotation.rs`, `c10.rs` and others). The owner-side supervisor already handles this race in `session_failure_result`, which maps a post-cancellation error to `Ok(())`; the M2 actor loop lacks the equivalent. **Remaining.** Map an error raised after the cancellation token fired to a stopped outcome, with a regression that stops while a forget barrier is pending and the carrier writer has exited and requires `stop()` to return `Ok` with the closed reason `stopped`. | — |
| M7-C85 | [ ] Reclaim the OPEN journal entry of a consumer-cancelled exchange. | planned | unassigned (defect found by the m3c7 reviewer) | **Defect.** Narrower relative of M7-C82, and not introduced by its fix: in both runs of `verify-m3-http-forward-real-path` four stream IDs are allocated before the sequential phase and only three are reclaimed, the unreclaimed one being the cancelled `/events` exchange. If the owner never issues `STREAM_FORGET` for an exchange the consumer cancelled — for example because `owner_stream_forget_state` never sees both terminals acknowledged after `RESET(CANCELLED)` — each cancellation permanently consumes one of the connector's 128 journal entries, so an unattended session still wedges after 128 cancellations. The M7-C82 claim of an unbounded number of sequential streams holds for exchanges that complete. **Remaining.** Establish whether a cancelled exchange is reclaimed at all, by extending the gate's wait or asserting `open_journal_entries` returns to 0 at run end; if it is not, fix the owner's terminal accounting and regress it. | — |
| M7-C86 | [ ] Land the peer-trust retention split: it fixes the isolation blackout and regresses `verify-m7-trust-expiry`. | planned | mcp (split out of M7-C83) | **Held back deliberately, on branch `m3c10-isolation-flake`, not landed.** This row exists so the work is not lost and not landed by accident. It is **M3-04's remaining blocker**.<br><br>**What it fixes.** A relay withdrew every approved peer SPKI pin whenever its membership runtime left `Ready`, which one failed reconcile during a case-boundary re-sign is enough to trigger. The transport pin watcher then closed every pooled peer connection and refused every fresh dial until the next refresh tick republished, so a consumer request landing in that window was answered `503 PEER_UNAVAILABLE` with an `unknown` execution. The split narrows withdrawal, on a recorded owner decision, to *rejected trust evidence* (`UnknownAuthority`, record-verification `MembershipRejected`, `CheckpointExpired`), while a *local or transient* unready state (`MissingLocalMembership`, `MissingLocalKey`, catalog, persistence, cancellation) retains the verified set and withdraws readiness instead, which is what the readiness contract in [cluster.md](cluster.md) already required. `MissingLocalKey` was split out as its own reason because a relay's own certificate not being an approved key of its own record is a statement about its right to serve, not about the peer keys it verified. With it, `verify-m3-mcp-isolation` is **12 of 12** with the race firing about twice per run.<br><br>**What it costs.** `verify-m7-trust-expiry` regresses. Measured interleaved on one machine with both binaries pre-built so no compilation ran during the measurement, and the load average recorded before every run: **base 10 of 10, branch 6 of 10** over ten pairs; a second interleaved series with timeline instrumentation gave base 6 of 6, branch 4 of 6. The failure is always the same shape — sixteen of the gate's seventeen joined conditions hold, and only `ingress_last_receive` differs, `Closed` where `TrustExpired` is required.<br><br>**Three hypotheses, all refuted, none by argument.** (1) *Closure is an accident of the pin layer.* No: the owner records `cause=PeerMembershipExpired`, `stream_removed=true` and one terminal event in **every** run, passing or failing. (2) *Attribution should key on the shared signed boundary `VerifiedPeerBinding::valid_until`.* Implemented as `c8ec3fb` and reverted as `f0bded1`: the trust/checkpoint window, already carried monotonically by `AdmissionDeadline::expires_at`, is *earlier* — 11:42:26.741 against 11:43:21.399 on one admission — so that arm can only fire later than the arm that already exists. (3) *The retention delays or drops the invalidation the dispatcher needs.* No: `dispatch_invalidations` instrumented identically on both revisions produces **identical traces** in base and branch, passing and failing runs — one `TrustExpired` per direction on the affected edge in every case.<br><br>**What the timeline shows, and why it is not yet a fix.** Correlating the ingress's recorded outcome against its own invalidation latch on one microsecond timeline, over six interleaved runs per arm: every passing run records `TrustExpired` with the latch within about 2 ms of the record, in either order; both failing runs record `Closed` with the latch 2.0 ms and 54.4 ms *after* it. But the ordering does not separate the two cleanly — one base run passed with the latch 2.4 ms after the record, carried by the monotonic-deadline arm — so the discriminator is not "latch before record" but whether *either* evidence arm held at the instant of observation, and in the failing runs neither did: the reset arrives before this relay's trust deadline. Making the ingress attribute expiry there would invent the evidence the contract forbids. The remaining question is structural — why the reset precedes the deadline on the branch and follows it at base — not a small local fix.<br><br>**Must not land until that is understood.** The principle is the owner's: a change that regresses a verified gate does not land to fix an unverified row. The gate-only work that does not touch retention is landed separately. | — |

| M7-I07 | [x] Exercise resource boundaries and cancellation under saturation: peer prefix/body/copy charge, stream/connection caps, queue backpressure, blackholed writes, and sibling-stream survival. | verified local | w-pressure worker, root validation | A new `verify-m7-queue-saturation` gate (integrated as b9d18b2 and 6a36114; root rerun in progress) proves physical message-queue occupancy through non-owner ingress relay-c against owner relay-a, replacing the earlier rejected logical-count evidence. The workload is derived from the actual bounds: a 4 MiB session budget, 128 message slots split across separate control and data channels, 64 streams per device, 65,536-byte bodies and a 131,072-byte initial window; each record charges the budget twice (retained replay chunk plus encoded frame), and because the ingress admits exactly one in-flight record per stream the reachable physical maximum is 64 entries, exactly half the slots, with the other half reserved by design for continuation, ACK and terminal frames; the previous 128-entry target was arithmetically unreachable at every admissible body size. With 20,000-byte records (61 percent of the budget across 64 streams) the gate observes 64 physically resident frames during a blackhole, a queue-bytes high-water of 2,544,540 corroborating the depth, control refusals of zero with the control enqueue count advancing during the blackhole, 65 reserved free data slots, cancellation plus fresh admission, sibling survival including the blackholed record's own echo, an immutable first terminal across eight samples, drain within two of 200 observations, three same-owner rotations starting on the exact paused carrier with one absolute deadline per attempt never extended, sockets at most three, and zero dispatch delta. New payload-free session diagnostics expose the queue-bytes limit and high-water, live control and data channel depth and capacity with high-water latches, refusals and enqueue counts; queueing behaviour is unchanged. Earlier Native50 lifecycle, Native45 peer capacity and Native49 owner-local capacity evidence stands. Corrected 2026-09-12 after an independent assessment: the remote-ingress body-limit rows closed with M7-C48 (9ec2452) and the reserved-capacity byte ambiguity with M7-C49 (fed782b), both verified, and `verify-m7-remote-body-limits` passes on the current source. The one remaining clause is late, reordered and duplicate frames and a peer GOAWAY on the saturated non-owner-ingress route, asserting an immutable terminal identity, a surviving sibling and no double delivery. Corrected 2026-09-15: the clause is now closed, including the sibling sub-clause across the GOAWAY, and the earlier diagnosis of why it could not be closed was wrong. That diagnosis held that the owner's planned drain is a listener-wide `GOAWAY(0)` which ends the device session within roughly 70 ms, so the post-FIN fence had to precede the GOAWAY for `INVALID_SEQUENCE` to stay attributable to the injected frame, and that closing the sub-clause needed either a per-connection drain seam or a second device session. The first half of that is right and the consequence is not: `GOAWAY(0)` closes **admission**, not an admitted session. Measured in this fixture on the current source: with the GOAWAY taken before the sibling round trip and before the fence, the session stays live across the drain, the sibling completes its own outstanding round trip byte exact, and the fence still closes the session with `INVALID_SEQUENCE` as its only session terminal event; with a deliberate one-second pause inserted after the GOAWAY the same sequence still passes, and the whole post-GOAWAY sequence measures 323..399 ms (`goaway_to_fence_ms`, bound 5,000 ms). The gate's phase order is therefore revocation, reorder, duplicate, **planned GOAWAY**, sibling, post-FIN fence; no product change was needed and none was made, and no second device session was needed. The fence remains the session's own last event, asserted rather than assumed by `session_terminal_events == 1` with the typed `INVALID_SEQUENCE` reason, which a drain never produces, together with the unchanged immutable first-terminal re-sampling. New payload-free evidence fields: `session_alive_after_goaway`, `fence_after_goaway`, `session_terminal_events`, `goaway_to_fence_ms`/`goaway_to_fence_bound_ms` and `sibling_round_trip_across_goaway`; `goaway_requested_after_fence`, which recorded the old limitation, is gone. One live red control on the current source proves the new across-GOAWAY assertions are not vacuous: with a five-second pause after the GOAWAY the drain has completed, the sibling's forward is reset (`H3_REQUEST_CANCELLED`) and the session closes `CONTROL_CLOSED` instead of `INVALID_SEQUENCE`, so the gate fails; each of the six new validator assertions also has a mutation case that passes the validator when its assertion is deleted. Four consecutive passes on the reordered gate: `session_alive_after_goaway=true fence_after_goaway=true session_terminal_events=1 goaway_to_fence_ms=323..399/5000 sibling_round_trip_across_goaway=true sibling_resident_frames=34..54 min_resident_during_injection=34..51 carrier_drain_records_consumed=15..32/48 saturation_window_ms=668..877/20000 elapsed_ms=946..1161`, with `verify-m7-ec044-peer-frames`, which shares the fixture, passing twice. `verify-m7-saturated-peer-frames` (`crates/tunnel-test-harness/src/peer_frames/saturated.rs`) closes the conjunction on one genuinely saturated non-owner-ingress route. It builds on the EC-044 fixture, which terminates the connector side itself, because the queue-saturation gate's carrier is a real CLI's mTLS socket behind a TCP proxy: forged, reordered or post-FIN frames cannot be placed on a TLS session the fixture does not terminate, and a device carrier has no peer GOAWAY seam. The device data carrier runs on its own peer client whose QUIC receive windows are deliberately small (192 KiB connection, 128 KiB stream - an environment bound, the analogue of the queue-saturation gate's 1 KiB proxy receive buffer), so when the fixture stops reading, the owner's physical writer blocks and its bounded outbound data channel fills; control and every consumer stream run on a second, ordinarily windowed connection. The unchanged queue-saturation workload is used: 20,000-byte records, one in-flight record per stream, the full 64-stream per-device cap proven by a refusal at cap plus one, and a required physical residency floor of `max_queue_messages / 4`. Evidence from four consecutive runs: `streams_admitted=64 stream_cap_refused_one_more=true data_queue_depth_high_water=52 physically_resident_frames=53 resident_floor=32 min_resident_during_injection=46..52 reserved_free_data_slots_at_peak=76 queue_bytes_high_water=2244475 data_bytes_high_water=2244475 control_bytes_available_at_data_peak=1949829 carrier_drain_records_consumed=14..22/48 saturation_window_ms=655..782/20000 revocation_close_delivered=true revocation_close_reason=grant-unavailable revocation_failure_code=GRANT_UNAVAILABLE revocation_stream_terminal=true revocation_dispatch_delta=0 data_queue_depth_at_revocation=52 control_enqueues_during_saturation=1 control_queue_refusals_during_saturation=0 reorder_recv_after_gap=0 reorder_delivered_after_gap=0 reorder_delivered_after_fill=2 reorder_order_restored=true duplicate_delivered_before=1 duplicate_delivered_after=1 duplicate_adapter_records=1 no_double_delivery=true late_fin_cursor=2 late_stream_terminals=1 late_adapter_bytes_after_fin=0 late_session_reason=INVALID_SEQUENCE terminal_identity_immutable=true terminal_identity_samples=8 sibling_survived_terminals=true goaway_sent_by_owner=true goaway_refused_new_peer_stream=true goaway_refusal_class=goaway`. Six live red controls fail without their assertion: delivering the reordered pair in order (`reorder_recv_after_gap,reorder_delivered_after_gap`), withholding the post-FIN frame (the session never closes), leaving the grant live (no revocation close arrives), never requesting the planned drain (the owner never reports a GOAWAY), dropping the workload to four fill streams (the residency floor is not reached), and replacing the duplicate with a genuinely new sequence (the session fences `INVALID_SEQUENCE` as unsolicited). A 53-case and an 18-case C17 validator mutation test cover every flag, count and bound. Two structural facts are recorded rather than worked around. `handle_peer_device_data` services one forwarded carrier from a single `tokio::select!` loop, so while its physical write is parked on QUIC flow control it is not reading: a fully blackholed forwarded carrier cannot deliver an inbound frame at all. The gate therefore consumes a bounded, counted drain allowance (at most 48 already-queued frames) only when an injected frame has not yet been accounted, and asserts the *minimum* residency observed at every injection step stayed above the same floor, so saturation is measured continuously rather than once. And `invalidate_stream_challenge` arms a five-second fail-closed terminal-FIN window that clears only when the stream is physically removed, so the fixture answers the revocation close the way a connector does, with its own `FIN` and an acknowledgement of the owner's terminal. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-028, EC-029, EC-030, EC-032, EC-033, EC-034, EC-036, EC-037, EC-038, EC-043, EC-044, EC-045, EC-046, EC-047, EC-049, EC-051, EC-052, EC-060, IN-01, IN-06](m7-task-tests.md#m7-i07). **Narrowed 2026-09-14.** The frame half is now proven on a real ingress-to-owner forward by `verify-m7-ec044-peer-frames` (reorder with the cursor held then restored, duplicate with no second delivery, late-after-FIN with exactly one terminal, zero adapter bytes past the FIN and a typed `INVALID_SEQUENCE` close), and `verify-m7-queue-saturation` separately asserts a surviving sibling and an immutable first terminal observation. What no gate asserts is the conjunction this row names: the frame gate runs one idle admitted session with a single outstanding record, no sibling and no GOAWAY, while the GOAWAY gate is an unsaturated planned drain with no injected frames. Remaining clause: inject a reordered, a duplicate and a post-FIN late frame plus a peer GOAWAY on the non-owner-ingress route while the data channel is blackholed at the physical ceiling, asserting one immutable terminal identity, a surviving sibling and no second adapter record. Fixture work only; no product change is implied. **IN-01 investigated and the gate corrected 2026-09-14 (e29af7d).** The concurrent-load gate failed about one run in three standalone and every run under contention, with a few of its 128 attempts returning `503 PEER_UNAVAILABLE` instead of a typed capacity refusal. The relay was not at fault and was not changed. That status is the owner's bounded pre-dispatch refusal while it quiesces admission for a scheduled rotation, which is documented behaviour, and the gate configures a three-second rotation on the very device it loads, so a quiesce window inside the load window is ordinary rather than exceptional. Attribution was established by instrumenting every candidate source: in eight consecutive contended failures the count of rotation-freeze refusals equalled the gate's reported transport failures exactly, with zero transport-capacity errors observed. The defect was in the gate's own measurement, which folded a typed, bounded, not-dispatched refusal into `transport_failures` under the cause `unexpected_http_status` and so broke the very class-separation invariant this row is about. The refusal is now counted as its own class, recognised only on its complete shape (status, code, `execution=not_dispatched`, retryable, and a retry hint that is present and bounded), and the asserted contract is that every attempt lands in exactly one class with reconnecting, transport, timeout and unknown all zero. That is narrower than before, not wider: a refusal with an absent or unbounded retry hint now fails where it previously passed as a transport failure. Three red controls cover an unaccounted attempt, an absent retry hint and one beyond the bound. A second defect surfaced in the same work: merging the load waves silently dropped the new counters, so 28 attempts belonged to no class at all. The merge destructures now, making a forgotten class a compile error. **Product fix from the same investigation:** ingress-local transport capacity reported `execution: unknown`. Every site that raises it does so strictly before anything is written to the owner, so the request provably never reached the owner; claiming uncertainty the relay does not have denied a consumer the retry a safe request is entitled to. Its regression fails without the change with left `unknown`, right `not_dispatched`. Gate result: ten consecutive passes, where it was ten consecutive failures, with the quiesce refusals visible in the evidence between 0 and 20 per run, each carrying a 250 ms retry hint. **Verified 2026-09-15.** The last residual, a sibling surviving across the owner's planned peer GOAWAY, is closed, and closing it corrected the diagnosis that had been recorded against it. That note said the drain ends the session within about seventy milliseconds so the sibling could not be observed across the GOAWAY while the post-FIN fence stayed the session's last event. A GOAWAY of zero closes admission, not an admitted session: already-admitted carriers keep serving through the drain, measured three ways including with a deliberate pause after the GOAWAY and by finding the session still in the owner's snapshot twenty seconds in. The seventy milliseconds was the previous run's own fence timing. The sibling's round trip now runs after the owner has written the GOAWAY and is refusing fresh peer streams, with session liveness sampled before the round trip rather than inferred from it, and the fence asserted to be the session's single terminal event carrying a typed invalid-sequence close that a drain never produces. No second device session and no product change were needed. Six new assertions each fail when deleted, and a live control confirms they are not vacuous: a five-second pause after the GOAWAY lets the drain finish, the sibling's forward is reset and the session closes as a control close instead, failing the gate. **Two limits recorded rather than smoothed over:** what tears the admitted carriers down between one and five seconds after the GOAWAY is not identified, and the residency minimum touched 34 against a floor of 32 in one of four runs, which is the tightest number in this evidence. | 2026-09-15T15:20:00+10:00 |
| M7-I08 | [x] Exercise same-owner rotation with active synthetic adapter streams while preserving control, logical stream IDs, fids/operation IDs, fences, and the socket bound. | verified local | w-goaway and w-i08 workers, root validation | Planned peer GOAWAY during same-owner rotation passes four consecutive times (integrated as 8eeb475) with exact candidate correlation, typed pre-dispatch classification, readiness withdrawal on an observed GoAway, stream-local drain joining, `post_goaway_dispatch_delta=1`, `later_request_dispatch_delta=0`, CLI survival, socket high-water 3 and joined cleanup. The three-failed-attempt scope is now closed by a new `verify-m7-i08-recovery-attempts` gate (integrated as 5b82046 and 38af0c0), which cuts the active carrier at the opaque fanout and then fails each recovery attachment by withholding the candidate handshake response after the client's upgrade, so the protocol's real candidate-loss retry path runs: `attempts=[1,2,3]` with backoff gaps of 156 ms and 233 ms above their 100 ms and 200 ms floors, every attempt deadline inside one 30-second episode budget that never restarts, distinct candidate routes and relays, owner attachment and release observed per attempt, cursors never rewound, a typed terminal failure after the third with the owner's own terminal reason, zero dispatch delta, no reconnect storm, owner socket high-water 2, a stable process id and joined cleanup; the positive episode recovers on attempt 2 with the same session, stream and operation identities and one dispatch. Nine mutation cases cover the attempt count, restarted or regressed deadlines, backoff, per-field terminal mismatches, reconnect storms, the socket bound, rewound cursors and retry exactness. Three real client defects were found and fixed red-then-green across this row's work, the third being the terminal-diagnostic race recorded as M7-C61: a control RESUME could win the select against the candidate data socket's handshake and the connector killed the session because snapshots arrived before the carrier, so replay queuing and snapshot replies now defer until the carrier installs; and a retry's RECOVERY_BEGIN was rejected on clock skew because the coordinator's remaining sample was compared against the connector's own budget even though the retained absolute deadline was already authoritative, nondeterministically killing attempt 2 or 3, so the sample is now clamped per the protocol's shorten-but-never-extend rule. Corrected 2026-09-12 after an independent assessment: the cross-relay handover racing peer grace and owner readiness is now exercised by `verify-m7-ec025-handover` (d6fd17e), so that gap is closed. The partial-response clause is now closed by a new `verify-m7-i08-partial-response-rotation` gate, with its wording renegotiated against what the product implements. Reading the code first established that byte-cursor resumption *inside* a record does not exist and is deliberately excluded: `M2Actor::emit_payload` (`crates/tunnel-client/src/m2_runtime.rs:6020-6043`) chunks a response with `output.chunks(MAX_PAYLOAD_LEN)` and reaches the carrier through the synchronous `emit_output_now`, so the single-task connector actor never yields between one response's frames and a rotation freeze can only land before or after a whole response; a frozen actor defers whole `PendingOutput` values into `pending_outputs` with no sequence allocated and flushes them whole onto the replacement carrier; the relay's fence is `StreamFence { last_emitted }`, a frame sequence number (`crates/tunnel-relay/src/actor.rs:6911-6939`), and `validate_ack_cursors` (`crates/tunnel-protocol/src/rotation.rs:2020-2052`) admits COMMIT only when the peer's `recv_contiguous` equals that fence exactly; the retained replay map is `BTreeMap<u64, Frame>` keyed by sequence and replays intact frames with only epoch/generation rewritten. No byte-offset-within-record field exists in `DirectionState`, `StreamFence`, `DrainProof` or `ReplayRange`, and `preflight_receive` rejects a byte-shifted re-send as `DuplicateConflict`. The gate therefore asserts what the product does guarantee, measured at the public consumer: a maximum-size 65536-byte synthetic record whose echoed response is 65579 bytes and necessarily spans two tunnel DATA frames is driven continuously across three same-owner rotations; every rotation is bracketed by a response in flight (`bracketing_commit=3`), each rotation leaves the response only partly delivered at an exact consumer byte cursor when the replacement generation becomes active (`partial_resume_offsets=[65536, 65536, 65536]` on every run), delivery then resumes from that cursor with `cursor_gaps=0` and `duplicated_bytes=0` because the received prefix is compared against the source prefix after every chunk, and each reassembled response matches a SHA-256 taken from the source envelope rather than from the delivery path (`checksums_matched=responses_delivered`, 10-12 per run). Each rotation proves a whole-frame drain with `relay_fence==relay_ack` and `connector_fence==connector_ack`, and runs where the relay outran the connector's ACK retained and replayed whole frames onto the replacement carrier (`replay_frames=2` per rotation, `rotations_retaining_replay` 0-3 depending on timing) rather than resuming a record at an offset. The adapter is then shut down inside the rotation overlap (`adapter_shutdown_phase=preparing`, graceful SIGINT exit) and the one post-shutdown probe lands in the closed vocabulary (`post_shutdown_outcome=closed_before_response`), with a partly delivered response never accepted as a complete one. Corrected 2026-09-12 after an independent assessment: configured saturation is covered by `verify-m7-queue-saturation`, and the M2 default plan with three actual 300-second rotations plus the M2 fault plan both pass on the current source. The remaining clause is the typed closure matrix: `StreamTerminalCause` still has exactly one variant (`PeerMembershipExpired`) and every other close carries an untyped static reason, so one gate must hold a stalled physical write past the relay's own deadline and assert a typed cause distinguishing queue exhaustion, delayed credit, physical write timeout, lease expiry and planned drain, latched before state is unregistered, with joined cleanup. **Edge-case tests:** [EC-027, EC-028, EC-029, EC-032, EC-038, EC-055, EC-057, EC-061, IN-05, IN-09, OG-08](m7-task-tests.md#m7-i27). **Re-confirmed 2026-09-14 at this commit, not merely restated:** `StreamTerminalCause` still defines exactly one variant, `PeerMembershipExpired`, every producer in the tree sets that one variant, and `StreamTerminalEvent.reason` remains an untyped static string. The chaos gate's interruption classes are a harness-side classification over those untyped reasons and so cannot satisfy a typed product cause. This row therefore needs a product change before any gate can assert it: distinct variants for queue exhaustion, delayed credit, physical write timeout, lease expiry and planned drain, attributed at each close site, then one gate that stalls a physical write past the relay's own deadline and asserts the typed cause latched before STREAM_FORGET with joined cleanup. It overlaps the EC-061 ordering clause, which needs the unregister stamped from the same diagnostic clock as the fault tuple. **Closed 2026-09-14 (5f4153b).** `StreamTerminalCause` gains `QueueExhausted`, `DelayedCredit`, `PhysicalWriteTimeout`, `AdmissionLeaseExpired` and `PlannedDrain` beside the existing variant, each attributed at a close site whose condition is established from the code, with an explicit cause from the caller always beating the actor's own fallbacks. Nine deterministic regressions, one per cause plus negatives, including a stalled physical write that latches its typed cause, proves the latch immutable under a second close with a different cause, then drives the connector FIN and asserts exactly one STREAM_FORGET with no surviving stream state. Relay lib 320 tests and workspace 1019 pass, strict clippy clean, and the four gates that read terminal events (queue saturation, trust expiry, chaos, owner loss) pass unchanged. **Judgement recorded so a reader can disagree:** the clause asked for one harness gate; the evidence is deterministic lib regressions instead, which prove each cause more precisely than a gate could, and the one thing they cannot reach is the public handler's own call of `terminal_cause`, since no lib test constructs a real axum WebSocket. That call site is covered by the mapping test and by reading, not by a socket. **Five conditions were deliberately not attributed** rather than guessed: the private listener's planned drain is indistinguishable from a normal peer close without a behaviour change, the ingress-side forwarding timeout has no stream registration, owner catalog lease loss closes the session and publishes no stream latch (so the variant is named `AdmissionLeaseExpired` for the lease it actually proves), and `write_echo_stream`'s resource refusals do not themselves close a stream. **Two mutation results are on record.** Removing the live-carrier guard, which would label a close on a lost carrier as queue exhaustion, passed the entire suite until a discriminating test was added; building it established that a tokio `Sender` whose `Receiver` was dropped reports its buffer free rather than full, so only the byte-budget arm can tell the two apart. And dropping the pending-records half of the delayed-credit condition still passes, so that guard is defensive code no test distinguishes from its absence; it was kept as the correct invariant and named rather than implied. One API deletion: `RelayHandle::close_echo_stream` lost its last caller and was removed, called out in the commit message so it is not later mistaken for an accident. | 2026-09-14T16:20:00+10:00 |
